Optimal low-rank posterior covariance approximation in linear Gaussian inverse problems on Hilbert spaces
Giuseppe Carere, Han Cheng Lie

Abstract
For linear inverse problems with Gaussian priors and Gaussian observation noise, the posterior is Gaussian, with mean and covariance determined by the conditioning formula. The covariance is the central object for uncertainty quantification, as it encodes the variability of the posterior distribution and thus the uncertainty in the posterior mean estimate. Using the Feldman–Hajek theorem, we analyse the prior-to-posterior update and its low-rank approximation for infinite-dimensional Hilbert parameter spaces and finite-dimensional observations. We show that the posterior distribution differs from the prior on a finite-dimensional subspace, and construct low-rank approximations to the posterior covariance, while keeping the mean fixed. Since in infinite dimensions, not all low-rank covariance approximations yield approximate posterior distributions which are equivalent to the posterior and prior distribution, we characterise the low-rank covariance approximations which do yield this equivalence, and their respective inverses, or ‘precisions’. For such approximations, a family of measure approximation problems is solved by identifying the low-rank approximations which are optimal for various losses simultaneously. These loss functions include the family of Rényi divergences, the Amari α \alpha -divergences for α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , the Hellinger metric and the Kullback–Leibler divergence. Our results extend those of Spantini et al. (SIAM J. Sci. Comput. 2015) to Hilbertian parameter spaces, and provide theoretical underpinning for the construction of low-rank approximations of discretised versions of the infinite-dimensional inverse problem, by formulating discretisation independent results.



Keywords
: nonparametric linear Bayesian inverse problems, Gaussian measures, low-rank operator approximation, generalised operator eigendecomposition, equivalent measure approximation

MSC codes
: 28C20, 47A58, 60G15, 62F15, 62G05

1
Introduction

The class of Bayesian inverse problems with linear forward models and Gaussian priors plays a special role in the context of Bayesian statistical inference. For example, this class of linear Gaussian inverse problems appears naturally in the Laplace approximation of posteriors for nonlinear statistical inverse problems, and the classical Kalman filter can be understood as an iterative solution method for a sequence of linear Gaussian inverse problems.
A particularly attractive feature of the class of linear Gaussian inverse problems is the availability of a closed-form solution, in the case where the parameter space is a separable Hilbert space. In this case, given a linear forward model
G
with codomain
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a realisation
y
of the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued data random variable

Y=GX+\zeta,\quad\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}),

and given a Gaussian prior
\mu_{\textup{pr}}=\mathcal{N}(m_{\textup{pr}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
for the unknown parameter
X
, the solution
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
to the Bayesian inverse problem is a Gaussian measure
\mathcal{N}(m_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}})
. The posterior mean
m_{\textup{pos}}
and the posterior covariance
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
can be computed explicitly:

\displaystyle m_{\textup{pos}}=m_{\textup{pr}}+\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}G^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1}(y-Gm_{\textup{pr}}),\quad\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}+G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*})^{-1}G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}},

see e.g.
[
36
, Example 6.23]
. It should be noted that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
does not depend on the realisation
y
of
Y
.

The availability of closed-form solutions to linear Gaussian inverse problems endows these problems with structure that makes them interesting objects to study in the context of measure approximation problems.
Measure approximation problems have become ubiquitous in modern statistical inference, often because one cannot sample exactly from the probability measure of interest, e.g. for computational cost reasons, or because one has only partial information about the measure of interest.
In the context of Bayesian inverse problems, we can also consider measure approximation problems as a way to analyse the Bayesian prior-to-posterior update.

Computational studies of Bayesian inverse problems on high- but finite-dimensional parameter spaces show that the data is often ‘informative’—i.e., that the posterior differs from the prior—only on a subspace of much lower dimension than the dimension of the parameter space; see e.g.
[
16
]
. In
[
12
]
, a similar subspace is called a ‘likelihood-informed subspace’. Since the posterior is obtained by reweighting the prior by the likelihood, this likelihood-informed subspace is determined by how the concentration of the likelihood interacts with the concentration of prior.
In the case of linear Gaussian inverse problems, the concentration of the likelihood and the concentration of the prior are described by the eigenpairs of the Hessian of the negative log-likelihood, referred to simply as ‘Hessian’ in this work, and the eigenpairs of the prior precision. These ideas are used in
[
16
]
to identify low-rank approximations of the posterior covariance matrix. In
[
35
]
, the optimality of these posterior covariance approximations with respect to a family of spectral loss functions is shown. In particular, the leading generalised eigenvectors of the matrix pencil defined by the Hessian and the prior precision build a hierarchy of nested low-dimensional subspaces on which the posterior differs from the prior.
If only a few directions in the parameter space need to be stored to be able to approximate the posterior distribution well, then this can be done before observing the data. Indeed, these directions are independent of the data, because the prior precision and the Hessian are independent of the data. In high but finite-dimensional parameter spaces, this leads to considerable computational and storage savings. Nowadays, the latter is important since read and write operations from memory often form the bottleneck in modern computational hardware, c.f.
[
27
]
.

So far, the existence of optimal low-rank approximations and likelihood-informed subspaces for linear Gaussian prior-to-posterior updates has only been proven for posterior distributions on finite-dimensional parameter spaces. Such low-rank approximations are exploited in
[
7
,
8
]
to obtain computationally tractable uncertainty quantification in high-dimensional inverse problems. In these works it is noticed that the spectral decay of the Hessian of a discretised and linearised version of an inverse problem seems independent of the discretisation dimension. As a consequence, also the spectral decay of the prior-preconditioned Hessian is independent of the discretisation dimension. This observation is central in the effort of making the resolution of the inverse problem scalable. In order to provide theoretical underpinning for this behaviour, it is fundamental to formulate the approximation procedure centered around the prior-preconditioned Hessian directly on the native infinite-dimensional space.

Analysing low-rank posterior covariance approximations in infinite dimensions adheres to the principle proposed in
[
36
]
of studying Bayesian inverse problems in their native function space formulation, while deferring the discretisation to the very end. A constructive formulation of optimal low-rank posterior covariance approximations in infinite dimensions can then be used to show that constructed discretised counterparts are consistent under increasingly fine discretisations levels, for a given inverse problem of interest. This is in parallel to showing the consistency of discretisations of the full posterior, see for example
[
31
]
. Consistent discretisations of optimal low-rank posterior covariance approximations are dimension robust, meaning that the posterior covariance approximation error in a discretised setting does not blow up under discretisation refinement but converges to the optimal approximation error in the infinite-dimensional setting.

We therefore formulate and solve the low-rank posterior covariance approximation problem of
[
35
]
, which provides a finite-dimensional analysis, instead in infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert spaces. While
[
36
, Example 6.23]
provides a formulation of the linear Gaussian inverse problem in infinite dimensions, in the generalisation of the optimal low-rank posterior covariance approximation analysed by
[
35
, Section 2]
certain challenges appear.

1.1
Challenges in infinite dimensions

In the finite-dimensional context of
[
35
]
, the above equation updating
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
provides a starting point for the approximation procedure. Also the corresponding equation which updates
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}
, c.f.
[
36
, eq. (6.13a)]
, provides a starting point for the approximation. These are called the ‘prior precision’ and ‘posterior precision’ respectively. An operator pencil involving
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
is central in the result of
[
35
, Theorem 2.3]
. When the prior distribution is nondegenerate, these can be interpreted as full-rank matrices, that is, finite-dimensional, hence bounded, linear operators. In infinite dimensions,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}
are no longer bounded, and they are not even defined on the entire parameter space. In fact,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
, the range of the self-adjoint square root of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
, is called the ‘Cameron–Martin space’ of the prior distribution and contains the domain of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
. This space is a proper subspace of the parameter space in infinite dimensions, and with probability 1, draws from the prior distribution do not belong to this space. This makes the required analysis of approximations based on the prior-to-posterior precision update more delicate.

Another complication of the infinite-dimensional setting is that, unlike in the finite-dimensional setting, not all approximations of the posterior mean and covariance result in approximate posterior measures that are equivalent to the exact posterior distribution, even if they have the same support; see the Feldman–Hajek theorem, recalled below in
Theorem

3.1
. Here, ‘equivalent’ means that the approximate posterior has a density with respect to the exact posterior distribution, and vice versa. Since the prior and the posterior distributions are equivalent for linear Gaussian inverse problems with finite-dimensional data, approximate posteriors which are not equivalent to the exact posterior are also not equivalent to the prior distribution. In fact, nonequivalent Gaussian measures on infinite-dimensional spaces are necessarily mutually singular. That is, they assign full measure to disjoint measurable sets, which is an undesirable property for the approximate posterior and exact posterior/prior distribution to have. Thus, an understanding of which approximate updates of the prior covariance lead to equivalent approximation posterior distributions, with probability 1 with respect to the data
Y
, is needed to construct approximate posterior measures equivalent to the exact posterior.

A third complication is that the analysis of the finite-dimensional setting in
[
35
]
relies on certain finite-dimensional results and concepts.
For example, in approximating the posterior covariance, a certain loss functional is used to measure the closeness of the approximate posterior covariance to the exact posterior covariance. The coercivity of this loss functional is used to prove some results in the finite-dimensional setting. However, in our infinite-dimensional formulation, the analogous coercivity statement does not hold.
Also, Fréchet differentiability of this loss functional, useful for finding extreme points of the loss, cannot be deduced in the same way as in the finite-dimensional case, as the latter case relies on the finite-dimensional result of
[
22
, Theorem 1.1]
.

1.2
Contributions

This work provides a rigorous analysis for the infinite-dimensional version of the Bayesian prior-to-posterior covariance and precision updates and constructs optimal low-rank approximations thereof. We assume a linear Gaussian inverse problem in which the parameter space is a possibly infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space, the observation space is finite-dimensional and the prior is nondegenerate and has mean zero. We identify optimal Gaussian approximations to the true posterior, keeping the mean fixed, using low-rank measure approximation problems. Our results extend the results of
[
35
]
that are developed for finite-dimensional parameter spaces, to the case where the parameter space is possibly infinite-dimensional. This shows a certain dimension independence of the results of
[
35
]
. In related work, see
[
9
]
, we study low-rank posterior mean approximation, and give some insight on joint posterior mean and covariance approximation. We highlight the main contributions of this paper.

The first main contribution is
Proposition

3.7
. In particular:

•

It formulates three operators and their relation in infinite dimensions. The three operators are important in the approximation procedure, and are given by the prior-preconditioned Hessian, the
posterior
-preconditioned Hessian and the posterior covariance preconditioned with the prior precision. As the relations are one-to-one, these operators contain the same information. It was already noted in previous works in finite dimensions, see e.g.
[
21
, Proposition 10]
and
[
20
, Section 3.4.1]
, that these operators and various other transformations of them contain the same information and are the central object for studying the quality of the finite-dimensional low-rank posterior approximation.

•

It gives one-to-one relations between the above three operators and the Hilbert–Schmidt operator which mixes the prior and posterior covariance in the Feldman–Hajek theorem, given by

\displaystyle R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})\coloneqq(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})^{*}-I.

(1)

The Feldman–Hajek theorem gives necessary and sufficient conditions for equivalence of Gaussian measures, and the connection with the three operators given here, shows that these operators essentially all quantify the amount of similarity and equivalence between the prior and the posterior distribution. This provides intuitive motivation for the importance of this family of operators in the study of optimal posterior approximation.

•

It shows that this family of operators can be diagonalised in the common Cameron–Martin space of the prior and the posterior. In particular, this implies that the diagonalisations of the above family of operators have interpretations as operator pencils as in the finite-dimensional case.

•

It shows that the prior and posterior distribution differ only on a finite-dimensional subspace, which is a subspace of the Cameron–Martin space of both the prior and posterior. Its dimension equals the rank of the Hessian of the negative log-likelihood, or equivalently, the rank of the forward model
G
.

The second main contribution is given by
Lemma

4.6
and
Proposition

4.7
. They are stated for an arbitrary Gaussian measure
\mu_{1}=\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
with
\mathcal{C}_{1}
injective. Among the low-rank updates of the covariance
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and the precision
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}
, these results characterise those low-rank updates which satisfy an equivalence property, namely that when keeping the mean fixed, they correspond to approximate distributions that are equivalent to
\mu_{1}
. Furthermore, these results also characterise the approximate precisions and covariances which correspond to respectively the low-rank covariance and precision updates satisfying this equivalence property.
In the Bayesian context,
Lemma

4.6
and
Proposition

4.7
also show that in infinite dimensions, not all updates of the prior covariance of the form considered in
[
35
]
satisfy this equivalence property, and not all updates of the prior covariance that do satisfy this property can be constructed as the inverse of an update of the prior precision considered in
[
35
]
. Our results give a necessary and sufficient condition on the range of the low-rank updates, under which such updates of the prior covariance do in fact satisfy the equivalence property. This provides a tool to inflate or deflate the covariance of a Gaussian measure while retaining access to Radon–Nikodym derivatives, e.g. to deflate prior covariance or inflate posterior covariance.

The third main contribution is to solve a family of Gaussian measure approximation problems in which we approximate the posterior covariance and keep the mean fixed, for example at the exact posterior mean. Using the main result mentioned above and other technical developments, the solutions are obtained in
Theorem

4.23
and
Corollary

4.26
, and can be stated informally as follows.

Theorem

(Informal version of
Theorem

4.23
and
Corollary

4.26
)
.

Let
r\leq n
, let
m\in\mathcal{H}
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
denote the prior and posterior covariances respectively. Let
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
be the Feldman–Hajek operator (
1
) and let
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})_{r}
be a truncated rank-
r
singular value decomposition of
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
. The following holds.

(i)

The covariance

\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}(I-[-R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})_{r}])\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}

minimises, over all covariances
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}>0
with linear
K:\mathbb{R}^{r}\rightarrow\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}\subset\mathcal{H}
, the loss

\displaystyle D\left(\ \mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*})\ \right),

for all
D(\cdot\|\cdot)
in a set of divergences, which includes the forward and reverse Kullback–Leibler divergence, the Rényi divergences of order
\rho
with
\rho\in(0,1)
, the Amari-
\alpha
divergences with
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and the Hellinger distance.

(ii)

With
(\lambda_{i},w_{i})_{i}\subset(-1,0]\times\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
the eigenpairs of
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}(-\lambda_{i})(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2})w_{i}\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}).

(iii)

The solution
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
is unique if and only if the following holds:
\lambda_{r+1}=0
or
\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r}+1
.

(iv)

The inverse of
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
is given by the unbounded operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left(\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}\right)(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i})\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}),

which is optimal among all precisions
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}
with linear
U:\mathbb{R}^{r}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, when measuring the approximation quality of the corresponding covariances
(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1}
using the losses considered in
item

(i)
.

The losses considered above are all spectral loss functions in the sense that when applied to any two Gaussian measures, they depend only on the spectrum of a suitable operator. The range condition
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
in
item

(i)
ensures that the resulting approximate posterior obtained by approximating the covariance and keeping the mean fixed is equivalent to the exact posterior. A precise statement on the optimal solutions for the covariance approximation problem, which includes an expression for the corresponding minimal loss, is provided in
Theorem

4.23
and
Corollary

4.26
.

In
[
35
]
, the optimal low-rank covariance approximation problem is analysed and solved in the finite-dimensional setting, in which
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}=\mathcal{H}
holds and the range condition
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
is always satisfied. The solution in
[
35
]
is formulated in terms of the eigenpairs of the prior-preconditioned Hessian instead of the Feldman–Hajek operator
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
defined in (
1
), but as mentioned above, these operators are transformations of each other by
Proposition

3.7
. The solution given in the infinite-dimensional version of
Theorem

4.23
reduces in the finite-dimensional setting to the solution found by
[
35
]
. For inverse problems with an infinite-dimensional parameter, it follows that
consistent
optimal posterior covariance approximations constructed via the approach of
[
35
]
are then dimension robust. Thus, besides obtaining an analysis of optimal low-rank covariance approximation of Gaussian posteriors on Hilbert spaces, our results also provide theoretical underpinning of the approximation method proposed in finite dimensions in
[
35
]
.

1.3
Related literature

Low-rank approximation of posterior covariance operators for linear Gaussian inverse problems posed on finite-dimensional parameter spaces is studied in
[
16
]
. In particular,
[
16
, eq. (5)]
presents a formula for a low-rank approximation of the posterior covariance that exploits spectral decay in the Hessian of the negative log-likelihood, and
[
16
, eq. (4)]
indicates that the error of this low-rank approximation is related to the tail of the spectrum of the prior-preconditioned Hessian of the negative log-likelihood.

In
[
35
]
, a precise formulation of the low-rank posterior covariance approximation problem is given and rigorously analysed, for linear Gaussian inverse problems on finite-dimensional parameter spaces. The low-rank approximation for the posterior covariance proposed in
[
16
]
is shown to be an optimal solution for a family of spectral loss functions that include as special cases the Kullback–Leibler divergence and Hellinger distance between Gaussians with the same mean but different covariances.
This approach is further developed for goal-oriented linear Gaussian inverse problems in
[
34
]
. Dimension reduction methods for linear Gaussian inverse problems using projections of the data are studied using generalised eigenvalue problems in
[
17
]
. The Kullback–Leibler divergence and mutual information are used to quantify the error of the approximating measures.

Dimension reduction for Bayesian inverse problems with possibly nonlinear forward models and non-Gaussian priors appears to have been first analysed in
[
38
]
. Joint dimension reduction of parameter and data is studied in
[
3
]
, for possibly nonlinear forward models and non-Gaussian priors. The results of
[
38
]
are further improved in
[
23
,
24
]
, which derived error bounds in terms of Amari
\alpha
-divergences

D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu\|\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha(\alpha-1)}\biggr(\int\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\mu-1\biggr),

for probability measures
\nu
and
\mu
such that
\nu\ll\mu
and
0<\alpha\leq 1
; see
[
23
, eq. (7)]
.
The above-cited works consider only the setting of finite-dimensional parameter spaces, and do not consider infinite-dimensional parameter spaces. While
[
35
]
provides explicit formulas for the approximation errors,
[
38
,
3
,
23
,
24
]
provide only error bounds. In infinite dimensions,
[
11
]
proposes a method for sampling the posterior based on the infinite-dimensional likelihood-informed subspace, and identifies the prior-preconditioned Hessian as the fundamental object. However, a rigorous treatment of optimality is not present.

In
[
28
]
, Kullback–Leibler approximation of probability measures on infinite-dimensional Polish spaces using Gaussians is studied from the calculus of variations perspective. The main results of this work concern existence of minimisers and convergence of a proposed minimisation scheme for identifying the best approximation in a class of approximating Gaussian measures. In our setting, the posterior is already Gaussian, and the approximation classes we consider differ from those in
[
28
]
.

In
[
1
, Section 3]
, importance sampling for linear Gaussian inverse problems posed on separable Hilbert spaces is considered. The main result is to identify two types of intrinsic dimension, such that if both dimensions are finite, then absolute continuity of the posterior with respect to the prior holds, and thus importance sampling is possible. Also
[
2
]
considers the setting of linear Gaussian inverse problems on separable Hilbert spaces. In this work, the aim is to analyse the Kullback-Leibler divergence from prior to posterior for optimal experimental design. The focus of our work is not to determine whether importance sampling is possible or study optimal experimental design, but rather to identify low-dimensional structure in the Bayesian prior-to-posterior update.

1.4
Outline

We introduce key notation in
Section

1.5
below. In
Section

2
, we begin by recalling the infinite-dimensional formulation of the linear Gaussian inverse problem, and formulate the posterior covariance and posterior precision approximation classes that define our measure approximation problems. In
Section

3
we recall the Feldman–Hajek theorem, which characterises when two Gaussian measures are equivalent, and recall expressions for the Kullback–Leibler divergence and Rényi divergence of two equivalent Gaussians. We state the first main result of this paper,
Proposition

3.7
, which identifies the generalised eigenpairs of the three operator pencils mentioned in the introduction and identifies the finite-dimensional subspace on which the posterior differs from the prior. In
Section

4
, we consider measure approximation problems where the posterior covariance is approximated and identify solutions in
Theorem

4.23
and
Corollary

4.26
. Auxiliary results are presented in
Appendix

A
, and proofs of the results in this work can be found in
Appendix

B
.

1.5
Notation

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space over
\mathbb{R}
, i.e. a linear space endowed with an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
which induces a complete topology and norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. Let
(e_{i})_{i}
be an orthonormal basis (ONB) of
\mathcal{H}
, where
i
ranges over a countable index set because
\mathcal{H}
is separable.
Let also
\mathcal{K}
be a separable Hilbert space over
\mathbb{R}
. By
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
,
\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
, and
\mathcal{B}_{00}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
, we denote the vector spaces of linear operators with domain
\mathcal{H}
and codomain
\mathcal{K}
that are bounded, compact, and finite-rank respectively, endowed with the operator norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
.
We define a finite-rank operator to be an operator that is bounded and has finite-dimensional range.
By
\mathcal{B}_{00,r}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
we denote the set of finite-rank operators that have rank at most
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
This set is not a vector space since the rank is not preserved under linear combinations.
If
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{H}
then we omit the second argument in the spaces above, e.g.
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\coloneqq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})
.
We write
L_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
to denote the vector spaces of trace-class and Hilbert–Schmidt operators, and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}
to denote their respective norms. We also equip
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
with the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}
.

For
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
, we denote the adjoint of
T
by
T^{\ast}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{H})
.
The space
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the space of bounded operators from
\mathcal{H}
to itself that are additionally self-adjoint. The spaces
\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{00}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
,
L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
and the set
\mathcal{B}_{00,r}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
for
r\in\mathbb{N}
are defined similarly.

For
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
we write
T\geq 0
and
T>0
if
T
is nonnegative or positive respectively, i.e. if respectively
\langle Th,h\rangle\geq 0
or
\langle Th,h\rangle>0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
.
If
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
is nonnegative, then
T^{1/2}
will denote its nonnegative self-adjoint square root, i.e.
T^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
T^{*}T
is self-adjoint and nonnegative for any
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we may define
\lvert T\rvert\coloneqq(T^{*}T)^{1/2}
.

For
h\in\mathcal{H}
and
k\in\mathcal{K}
, we interpret the tensor product
k\otimes h
as a rank-1 operator in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
, and this operator is
\tilde{h}\mapsto\langle h,\tilde{h}\rangle k
. For
T\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H,\mathcal{K}})
,
T
can be written in its ‘singular value decomposition’ as a series of rank-1 operators
T=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}k_{i}\otimes h_{i}
where
(\sigma_{i})_{i}
is nonincreasing and nonnegative and
(h_{i})_{i}
and
(k_{i})_{i}
are orthonormal sequences in
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
respectively, see also
Lemma

A.5
.

A linear operator
T:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
which is not necessarily bounded is said to be unbounded. Furthermore
T
is densely defined if its domain
\operatorname{dom}{T}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. We also write
T:\operatorname{dom}{T}\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
to emphasise the domain of definition of
T
. Thus, an unbounded linear operator
T:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
generalises the notion of
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
in two ways:
\operatorname{dom}{T}
may be a proper subspace of
\mathcal{H}
and
T
need not be bounded on
\operatorname{dom}{T}
.
If
T:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
,
S:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
and
U:\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
for some separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{Z}
, then
T+S:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
is defined on
\operatorname{dom}{T}\cap\operatorname{dom}{S}
and
UT:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
is defined on the preimage or inverse image
T^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}{U})
.

Self-adjoint unbounded operators are recalled in
Definition

A.18
and
Definition

A.20
.

For a densely defined linear operator
S:\operatorname{dom}{S}\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
with domain
\operatorname{dom}{S}\subset\mathcal{H}
,
an extension
T
of
S
is an operator defined on
\operatorname{dom}{T}\subset\mathcal{H}
, such that
\operatorname{dom}{S}\subset\operatorname{dom}{T}
and the restriction of
T
to
\operatorname{dom}{S}
agrees with
S
.
We shall write
S\subset T
to denote that
T
is an extension of
S
. If
T
is bounded, then
T
is the unique extension of
S
to all of
\mathcal{H}
, see
[
29
, Theorem I.7]
.

We let
\Lambda:L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be a function that sends a self-adjoint Hilbert–Schmidt operator to its square-summable eigenvalue sequence. One possible ordering labels the negative eigenvalues with the even integers and the positive eigenvalues with the odd integers, both ordered decreasingly in absolute value. A different choice is to order the eigenvalues in order of decreasing absolute value. Note that the choice of ordering of
\Lambda
in two operators
T,S\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
is allowed to be different. The precise ordering of eigenvalues that
\Lambda
assigns to an operator is not important, as we shall only consider compositions of
\Lambda
with functions on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
that are permutation invariant.
In analogy to the eigenvalue map
\Lambda:L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we define
\Lambda^{m}:L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
for any
m
-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{H}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
to be the map that sends
X\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
to its eigenvalue sequence, which is of length
m
, ordered in a nonincreasing way.

We denote equivalence of two measures
\mu
and
\nu
by
\mu\sim\nu
. That is,
\mu\sim\nu
if
\mu
and
\nu
are absolutely continuous with respect to each other. The measure
\nu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu
if
\mu(A)=0
implies
\nu(A)=0
for every measurable set
A
. We denote the support of a measure
\mu
by
\operatorname{supp}\mu
.

We write
X\sim\mu
to denote that the distribution of a random variable
X
is
\mu
. If
X
has a Gaussian distribution on
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.
\langle X,h\rangle
is a one-dimensional Gaussian random variable for each
h\in\mathcal{H}
, then we write
X\sim\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C})
, where
m=\mathbb{E}X
is the mean of
X
and
\langle\mathcal{C}h,k\rangle=\mathbb{E}\langle h,X-m\rangle\langle X-m,k\rangle
defines the covariance
\mathcal{C}
of
X
.
The ‘precision’ of
\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C})
is
\mathcal{C}^{-1}
.

For
I
a non-empty interval in
\mathbb{R}
,
\ell^{2}(I)
denotes the space of square-summable sequences, i.e.
\ell^{2}(I)=\{(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset I\ :\ \sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}<\infty\}
. If
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is open, then
C^{1}(I)
denotes the set of continuously differentiable functions on
I
.

We write ‘
a\leftarrow b
’ to denote the replacement of
a
with
b
.

2
Low-rank posterior covariance approximations

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space over
\mathbb{R}
of dimension
\dim{\mathcal{H}}\leq\infty
, which models the parameter space. Consider the observation model defined by a continuous linear forward model
G\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{R}^{n})
and additive Gaussian observation error

\displaystyle Y=Gx^{\dagger}+\zeta,\quad\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}).

(2)

The covariance
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})_{\mathbb{R}}
of the observation noise
\zeta
is a positive operator and
x^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{H}
.
By the Gaussian assumption on the noise, it follows that for any fixed
x\in\mathcal{H}
, the likelihood of observing
y
is proportional to
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1/2}(y-Gx)\rVert^{2})
. The Hessian of the negative log-likelihood with respect to
x
is

\displaystyle H=G^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1}G\in\mathcal{B}_{00,n}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}.

(3)

It follows from
H={G}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1/2}(G^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1/2})^{*}
that
H
is self-adjoint and nonnegative.

We adopt the Bayesian perspective to the problem of inferring
x^{\dagger}
given the observation
y
of
Y
, by modeling the unknown
x^{\dagger}
with an
\mathcal{H}
-valued random variable
X
. Its distribution, the prior distribution, is taken to be a Gaussian measure
\mu_{\textup{pr}}=\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
on
\mathcal{H}
and we assume that
X
and
\zeta
are independent. As the covariance of a Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
lies in
L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}\geq 0
, hence
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
has a unique nonnegative square root
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
. In this work, we make the following assumption.

Assumption 2.1
.

We assume that the prior distribution
\mu_{\textup{pr}}=\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
is nondegenerate on
\mathcal{H}
.

Nondegeneracy of
\mu_{\textup{pr}}
implies that
\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{\textup{pr}})=\mathcal{H}
, see e.g.
[
5
, Definition 3.6.2]
, and that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}>0
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}>0
, see
Lemma

A.23
. In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
are injective by
Lemma

A.4
. Hence the inverses
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}
are well-defined bijections
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pr}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
respectively. They are unbounded, self-adjoint operators, c.f.
Definition

A.18
and
Lemma

A.22
(ii)
. The Cameron–Martin space of
\mu_{\textup{pr}}
is the Hilbert space
(\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}})
, see e.g.
[
5
, p. 293]
, where the Cameron–Martin norm of an element
h\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
is defined by
\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}}\coloneqq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}h\rVert
. As
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
is injective and compact,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
and if
\dim{\mathcal{H}}=\infty
, then
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
is a proper dense subspace of
\mathcal{H}
.

A common way to construct covariance operators on function spaces is to consider inverses of Laplacian-like operators, c.f.
[
36
]
. This approach is used in computation; see e.g.
[
8
]
.

Given a realisation
y
of the random variable
Y
defined by the observation model, the posterior distribution
\mu_{\textup{pos}}=\mu_{\textup{pos}}(y)
of
X
given
Y=y
is the Gaussian measure
\mathcal{N}(m_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}})
, where

\displaystyle m_{\textup{pos}}=m_{\textup{pos}}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}G^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1}y\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}},

(4a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}+G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*})^{-1}G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}},

(4b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+G^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}^{-1}G=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+H,

(4c)

see e.g.
[
36
, Example 6.23]
. Equation (
4c
) should be understood to imply the following two facts:
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pos}}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}{(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+H)}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{\textup{pr}}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+H:\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is the inverse of the operator
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
given in (
4b
).
While all nondegenerate Gaussians are equivalent in a finite-dimensional setting, this is no longer true in an infinite-dimensional setting, where in fact it holds that nondegenerate Gaussians that are not equivalent must be mutually singular; see the Feldman–Hajek theorem, recalled in
Theorem

3.1
. By
[
36
, Theorem 6.31]
,
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mu_{\textup{pr}}
are in fact equivalent. In particular,
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
is a nondegenerate measure and the above properties of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
also hold for
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
.
We shall construct approximations to
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
that are equivalent to
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
.

The equations in (
4
) motivate certain Gaussian approximations of
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
that, as we shall see, retain equivalence to
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
. By (
4b
),
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
is an update of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
by a nonpositive self-adjoint operator
-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}+G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*})^{-1}G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
. The range of this update is contained in
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
and the rank of this update is at most
n
since
G\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, this motivates the rank-constrained approximation of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
by updating
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
using nonpositive self-adjoint operators of the form
-KK^{*}
, for
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
with
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}>0
. That is, we consider

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{r}\coloneqq\left\{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}>0:\ K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H}),\ \operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}\right\},\quad r\in\mathbb{N}.

(5)

Since
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}+G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}G^{*})^{-1}G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}\in\mathcal{B}_{00,n}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}
for all
r\geq r_{0}
by (
4b
), where
r_{0}\coloneqq\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}+G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}G^{*})^{-1}G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}\right)\leq n
.

Alternatively, we can consider approximations of
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
by constructing rank-constrained updates of the prior precision
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
. By (
4c
),
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}
is an update of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
by the Hessian
H
, which is self-adjoint, nonnegative, and has rank at most
n
. For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we can therefore consider the class of approximations of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}
of the form
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}
, for
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. That is, we consider

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{r}\coloneqq\left\{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}:\ U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\right\},\quad r\in\mathbb{N}.

(6)

Since
H\in\mathcal{B}_{00,n}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}
for all
r\leq r_{0}
with
{r_{0}}=\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
. The updates
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}
in (
6
) are defined on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
, by definition of the sum of unbounded operators, c.f.
Section

1.5
.

We note that every operator
SS^{*}
for
S\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
is a nonnegative, self-adjoint operator with rank at most
r
, and that every nonnegative operator
T\in\mathcal{B}_{00,r}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
can be written in this way. Therefore, we could write the above approximations as
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-T
or
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+T
for nonnegative
T\in\mathcal{B}_{00,r}(\mathcal{H})
, such that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-T
is positive and maps into
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
. However, the set of nonnegative elements of
\mathcal{B}_{00,r}(\mathcal{H})
is not convex, since rank is not preserved by convex combinations. By replacing
T
by
SS^{*}
, we avoid formulating an optimisation problem over a nonconvex set. Indeed,
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
is not only convex but is also a Banach space.

The classes
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
are generalisations to a possibly infinite-dimensional setting of those considered in
[
35
]
.
We search for low-rank approximations of the objects in (
4b
) and (
4c
), where ‘low-rank’ refers to the fact that we consider approximations in the classes
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
, for
r<n
respectively.
[
9
, Section 8]
contains two examples which can be analysed in the framework described in this section.

3
Equivalence and Divergences between Gaussian measures

Since our approximation problems are formulated in the context of statistical inverse problems, and since absolute continuity of the posterior with respect to the prior is important for statistical inference, we require our approximate posteriors to be equivalent to
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
. In
Section

3.1
, we recall the Feldman–Hajek theorem which gives necessary and sufficient conditions for Gaussian measures to be equivalent, and apply this theorem to the setting described in
Section

2
. Then, in
Section

3.2
, we consider certain divergences between equivalent Gaussian measures, which we use to measure the approximation quality of low-rank posterior approximations.

Unless otherwise specified, the proofs of the results below are given in
Section

B.1
.

3.1
Equivalence between Gaussian measures

Given a fixed nondegenerate reference Gaussian measure, the set of equivalent Gaussian measures is described by the Feldman–Hajek theorem, see e.g.
[
5
, Corollary 6.4.11]
or
[
13
, Theorem 2.25]
.

Theorem 3.1

(Feldman–Hajek)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
\mu=\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
and
\nu=\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})
be Gaussian measures on
\mathcal{H}
.
Then
\mu
and
\nu
are either singular or equivalent, and
\mu
and
\nu
are equivalent if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
,

(ii)

m_{1}-m_{2}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and,

(iii)

(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})^{*}-I\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.

The operator appearing in
Theorem

3.1
(iii)
quantifies the amount of similarity between Gaussian measures. If it does not have square-summable eigenvalues, then the Gaussian measures are mutually singular. In the other extreme, if the Gaussian measures are equal, then this operator is equal to 0 and the squared eigenvalues sum to 0.

Remark 3.2

(Cameron–Martin norm equivalence)
.

Theorem

3.1
states that the Cameron–Martin spaces
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{1/2}}
,
i=1,2
, of the Gaussian measures
\mu
and
\nu
are equal as subspaces if
\mu
and
\nu
are equivalent, see also
[
5
, Proposition 2.7.3]
. In fact, the two Cameron–Martin spaces
(\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{1/2}},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{-1}})
,
i=1,2
, must then have equivalent Cameron–Martin norms as well. This follows from
Lemma

A.14
applied to the square root of the two covariances. This fact is mentioned without proof in
[
28
, Proposition B.2]
and
[
6
, Proposition B.1]
.

Let us define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}:\ \mathcal{N}(m_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C})\sim\mu_{\textup{pos}}\},

(7)

and more generally, for
m_{1}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}:\ \mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C})\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\}.

(8)

That is,
\mathcal{E}
contains those covariances
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{N}(m_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C})
is equivalent to
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(m_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}})
.
Since
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mu_{\textup{pr}}
are equivalent, we have
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}\in\mathcal{E}
.

In order to characterise the set
\mathcal{E}
in (
7
), we introduce the following definition, which is closely related to
Theorem

3.1
(iii)
. This definition appears in
[
5
, Section 6.3]
.

Definition 3.3
.

If
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is invertible and
AA^{*}-I\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, then we say that
A
satisfies ‘property E’.

By
[
5
, Lemma 6.3.1(ii)]
the set of operators that satisfy property E is closed under taking inverses, adjoints and compositions.
Furthermore, since
\mathcal{\mu}_{\textup{pos}}\sim\mu_{\textup{pr}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
satisfies property E.
One can now use
Theorem

3.1
to describe the set
\mathcal{E}
in (
7
) explicitly, see
Lemma

A.24
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}&=\left\{\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}:\ \mathcal{C}>0,\ \mathcal{C}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\text{ satisfies property E}\right\}\\
&=\left\{\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}:\ \mathcal{C}>0,\ \mathcal{C}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\text{ satisfies property E}\right\}.\end{split}

(9)

For
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
, we now define

\displaystyle R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})^{*}-I.

(10)

By
Theorem

3.1
(iii)
,
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. Since
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})
is a self-adjoint compact operator, there exists an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
that diagonalises
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})
, see
Lemma

A.5
. We note that
R(\cdot\|\cdot)
is in general not symmetric in its arguments. The result below will be used frequently in our analysis of low-rank approximations of the posterior covariance operator.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
be injective covariances of equivalent Gaussian measures.
Then there exists a sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
and ONBs
(w_{i})_{i}
and
(v_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
such that
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}w_{i}
and the following statements hold:

(i)

\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}-I\subset(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})^{*}-I=R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})=\sum_{i=1}\lambda_{i}w_{i}\otimes w_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,

(ii)

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}-I\subset(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})-I=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}v_{i}\otimes v_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,

(iii)

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}-I\subset(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2})^{*}-I=R(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})=\sum_{i}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}v_{i}\otimes v_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,

(iv)

\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}-I\subset(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2})^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2})-I=\sum_{i}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}w_{i}\otimes w_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,

where the domains of the leftmost operators in each statement are dense and in
items

(i)
and
(iii)
contain
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}}
.

If
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are as given in
Lemma

3.4
, then the operator
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
is invertible, by
Theorem

3.1
and
Lemma

A.24
. Furthermore, the map
\lambda\mapsto\frac{-\lambda}{1+\lambda}
is a bijection on
(-1,\infty)
. Thus, each of the pairs
(\lambda_{i},w_{i})
,
(\lambda_{i},v_{i})
,
(\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}},v_{i})
and
(\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}},w_{i})
determines the other three. Hence,
Lemma

3.4
shows that the operator in
Theorem

3.1
(iii)
can equivalently be described by the operators in
items

(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
, which thus all contain the same information.
As explained in
Remark

3.6
, finding the eigenpairs of the operators in
Lemma

3.4
can be seen as a generalisation of finding the generalised eigenpairs of certain covariance operator pencils, which we first define below.

Definition 3.5
.

For possibly unbounded operators
T,S:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, the operator pencil
(T,S)
is defined by the collection of operators
\{T-\lambda S,\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}
. A ‘generalised eigenvalue’ of
(T,S)
is a value
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
for which
T-\lambda S
is not injective. For such
\lambda
there exists a nonzero
v\in\operatorname{dom}{T}\cap\operatorname{dom}{S}
such that
Tv=\lambda Sv
, which is called a ‘generalised eigenvector’, and we say that
(\lambda,v)
is a ‘generalised eigenpair’ of
(T,S)
.

Remark 3.6

(Generalised eigenpairs)
.

If
w_{i}\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
for some
i
, then the statement of
Lemma

3.4
(i)
implies
\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}w_{i}=(1+\lambda_{i})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}w_{i}
. In other words,
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}w_{i})=(1+\lambda_{i})\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}w_{i})
, showing that
(1+\lambda_{i},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}w_{i})
is a generalised eigenpair of the generalised operator pencil
(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{1})
. Furthermore, if
(v_{i})_{i}
lies in the dense subspace
\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
, then for any
i
we have
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}v_{i}\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}}
. The relation in
Lemma

3.4
(ii)
shows that
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}v_{i}=(1+\lambda_{i})v_{i}
, so that
v_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}v_{i}\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}}\cap\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1}}
. The previous relation implies
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}v_{i}=(1+\lambda_{i})\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}v_{i}
, showing that
(1+\lambda_{i},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}v_{i})=(1+\lambda_{i},\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}w_{i})
is a generalised eigenpair of
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1})
. Thus, in the case
(w_{i})_{i}
and
(v_{i})_{i}
lie in suitable dense subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
,
Lemma

3.4
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
,
(iv)
can be interpreted as statements on generalised eigenpairs of operator pencils, such as
(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{1})
or
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1})
. On the contrary, the diagonalisation of the operators in
Lemma

3.4
does not require such assumptions on the eigenvectors. Thus, the Feldman–Hajek operator
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})
in
Lemma

3.4
(i)
and its reformulations in
Lemma

3.4
(ii)
,
(iii)
,
(iv)
are the canonical objects to diagonalise when
\dim{\mathcal{H}}=\infty
, rather than covariance operator pencils.

Theorem

3.1
and
Lemma

3.4
hold for any equivalent Gaussian measures. In the specific case of the linear Bayesian inverse problem (
2
), in which case the posterior precision is a finite-rank update
H
of the prior by (
4c
), more can be said about the eigenvectors and eigenvalues given by
Lemma

3.4
of the operators
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}})
and
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
. We remind the reader of the definition of the Hessian
{H}
in (
3
).

Proposition 3.7
.

There exists a nondecreasing sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,0])
consisting of exactly
\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
nonzero elements and ONBs
(w_{i})_{i}
and
(v_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
such that
w_{i},v_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
and
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and

\displaystyle R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}w_{i}\otimes w_{i},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2})^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2})-I=\sum_{i}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}w_{i}\otimes w_{i},

(11a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=I-(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})=\sum_{i}(-\lambda_{i})v_{i}\otimes v_{i},

(11b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda_{i})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i},\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{N}.

(11c)

In
Proposition

3.7
,
w_{i},v_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
for all
i
, so that
v_{i}\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}
and
w_{i}\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
, because
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}}
. The equations (
11a
) and (
11b
) can be interpreted as statements on operator pencils by
Remark

3.6
.
More specifically, (
11a
) states that
(\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i})
is a generalised eigenpair of
(H,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1})
and (
11b
) states that
(-\lambda_{i},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}v_{i})=(-\lambda_{i},\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i})
is a generalised eigenpair of
(H,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1})
, for any
i
. Furthermore, (
11c
) can be interpreted as a statement on the operator pencils
(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
and
(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1})
. The prior-preconditioned Hessian
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
has been found to be the central object of study in the reduction of finite-dimensional linear Gaussian inverse problems, see
[
35
,
12
]
. We observe that this operator is directly related to
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
via the equivalent characterisations given by
Lemma

3.4
(i)
,
(ii)
(iii)
,
(iv)
and hence to the function
R(\cdot\|\cdot)
which quantifies the similarity of Gaussian measures by
Theorem

3.1
(iii)
and definition (
10
).

3.2
Divergences between equivalent Gaussian measures

To measure the quality of an approximation
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
, we shall use the Rényi divergences of order
\rho\in(0,1)
and the forward and reverse Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergences. The KL divergence from a measure
\nu_{1}
to a measure
\nu_{2}
equivalent to
\nu_{1}
is defined as

\displaystyle D_{\textup{KL}}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\log{\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}.

If
\nu_{2}
is a given measure that needs to be approximated and
\nu_{1}
is an approximation of
\nu_{2}
, then we refer to
D_{\textup{KL}}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
and to
D_{\textup{KL}}(\nu_{1}\|\nu_{2})
as the ‘forward’ and ‘reverse’ KL divergence of the approximation respectively. The Rényi divergence of order
\rho\in(0,1)
is defined by

\displaystyle D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})=-\frac{1}{\rho(1-\rho)}\log{\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}\right)^{\rho}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}},

c.f.
[
25
, eq. (130)]
.
It holds that
D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\nu_{1}\|\nu_{2})=D_{\textup{Ren},1-\rho}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
, because

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}\right)^{\rho}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}\right)^{1-\rho}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}\right)^{1-\rho}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}\right)^{\rho}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}\right)^{1-\rho}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}.

This is known as the ‘skew symmetry’ of the Rényi divergence, c.f.
[
37
, Proposition 2]
. Consequently, there is no need to consider forward Rényi divergences
D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
and reverse Rényi divergences
D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\nu_{1}\|\nu_{2})
separately.

In the Gaussian case, an explicit representation of these divergences holds, as shown in
[
25
]
. For this, we need a generalisation of the determinant to infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Because in infinite dimensions the eigenvalues of a compact operator accumulate at 0, direct extension of the finite-dimensional definition of the determinant as the product of the eigenvalues to infinite dimensions will result in the determinant function being equal to the constant
0
. A generalisation of the concept of the determinant for trace-class and Hilbert–Schmidt operators is given by the Fredholm determinant and Hilbert–Carleman determinant respectively. These are defined on respectively trace-class and Hilbert–Schmidt perturbations of the identity, and are indicated by
\det(I+A)
,
A\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, and respectively
\det_{2}{(I+A)}
,
A\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. We refer to
[
32
, Theorem 3.2, Theorem 6.2]
or
[
33
, Lemma 3.3, Theorem 9.2]
. For
A\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, we have
\det_{2}{(I+A)}=\det(I+A)\exp(-\operatorname{tr}\,\left({A}\right))
, and for
A\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
the determinants are related via
\det_{2}(I+A)=\det(I+(I+A)\exp(-A)).

By
[
32
, Theorem 4.2, Theorem 6.2]
or
[
33
, Theorem 3.7, Theorem 9.2]
for each
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, we have the expression

\displaystyle\det(I+\mu A)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}(1+\mu\lambda_{i}),\quad A\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})

(12)

\displaystyle\det\nolimits_{2}(I+\mu A)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}(1+\mu\lambda_{i})\exp{(-\mu\lambda_{i})},\quad A\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H}),

(13)

where
(\lambda_{i})_{i}
denotes the eigenvalue sequence of
A
.
In the case that
\dim{\mathcal{H}}<\infty
we note that
A-I\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and
\det(A)=\det(I+(A-I))=\prod_{i}\lambda_{i}
and thus
\det(\cdot)
indeed extends the finite-dimensional definition of the determinant. We can now formulate the explicit expression of the KL and Rényi divergences for equivalent Gaussian measures.
The result below holds when
\mathcal{H}
is a separable Hilbert space of finite or infinite dimension.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
be positive. If
m_{1}-m_{2}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and if
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}
satisfies property E, then

\displaystyle D_{\textup{KL}}(\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})\|\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(m_{2}-m_{1})\right\rVert^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\nolimits_{2}(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})),

(14a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})\|\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}))&=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bigr(\rho I+(1-\rho)(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))\bigr)^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(m_{2}-m_{1})\right\rVert^{2}\\
&+\frac{\log\det\left[\bigl(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\bigr)^{\rho-1}\bigl(\rho I+(1-\rho)(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))\bigr)\right]}{2\rho(1-\rho)}.\end{split}

(14b)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 1}D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})\|\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}))=D_{\textup{KL}}(\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})\|\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})),

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})\|\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}))=D_{\textup{KL}}(\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\|\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})).

The limits above show that for Gaussian measures, the Rényi divergence interpolates the forward KL, obtained in the limit
\rho\uparrow 1
, and the reverse KL, obtained in the limit
\rho\downarrow 0
.

Remark 3.9

(Amari
\alpha
-divergences and Rényi divergences)
.

For
\alpha\geq 0
, the Amari
\alpha
-divergences form another family of divergences which interpolates the forward KL at
\alpha=1
and reverse KL at
\alpha=0
, c.f.
[
23
, eq. (7)]
. For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\alpha>1
, the Amari
\alpha
-divergence
D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
for equivalent measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
on
\mathcal{H}
is defined by

\displaystyle D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})\coloneqq\frac{-1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\left(\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}\right)^{\alpha}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}-1\right),

and for
\alpha\in(0,1)
it is related to the
\rho
-Rényi divergence in (
14b
) with
\rho\leftarrow\alpha
by

D_{\textup{Ren},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})=\frac{-1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\log[1-\alpha(1-\alpha)D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})],

that is,

\displaystyle D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})=\frac{-1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\left(\exp[-\alpha(1-\alpha)D_{\textup{Ren},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})]-1\right).

(15)

Since
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are equivalent and
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
(\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}})^{\alpha}>0
with
\nu_{1}
-measure 1 and hence
1-\alpha(1-\alpha)D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})=\int(\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}})^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}
is strictly positive. It follows that
D_{\textup{Ren},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
is a strictly increasing function of
D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
. Thus, for every
0<\alpha<1
, minimising the
\alpha
-Rényi divergence corresponds to minimising the Amari
\alpha
-divergence, and vice versa.

Remark 3.10

(Hellinger distance)
.

Let us denote the Hellinger distance between equivalent measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
on
\mathcal{H}
by
D_{\textup{H}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
, i.e.

\displaystyle D_{\textup{H}}(\nu_{2},\nu_{1})^{2}\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}}\right)^{2}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}=2-2\int_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}}}\operatorname{d}\!{\nu}_{1}.

It holds that

\displaystyle D_{\textup{H}}(\nu_{2},\nu_{1})^{2}=2\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}D_{\textup{Ren},1/2}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})\right)\right),

(16)

by e.g.
[
25
, eqs. (134)–(135)]
, and it follows that minimising the Hellinger distance
D_{\textup{H}}(\nu_{2},\nu_{1})
is equivalent to minimising the Bhattacharyya distance
\frac{1}{4}D_{\textup{Ren},1/2}(\nu_{2}\|\nu_{1})
and vice versa.

4
Optimal approximations of covariance operators

In this section, we formulate a minimisation problem that aims at finding low-rank approximations of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
that are optimal simultaneously with respect to all members of a class of spectral loss functions. This class includes the Rényi divergences and forward and reverse KL divergences as special cases. The loss class and the low-rank covariance approximation problems are introduced in
Section

4.1
, the equivalence to the exact posterior of the approximations considered in
Section

2
is studied in
Section

4.2
, the approximation problems are formulated as minimisation problems involving a differentiable function in
Section

4.3
, and the approximation problems are solved in
Section

4.4
. A comparison with the finite-dimensional results of
[
35
]
is provided in
Section

4.5
.
The proofs of all the results in this section are given in
Section

B.2
.

4.1
Spectral loss functions and problem formulation

To measure the quality of a given approximation of the exact posterior covariance
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
, we define a class of loss functions on
\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}
in the following way. Recall the definition of the eigenvalue map
\Lambda
defined on Hilbert–Schmidt operators, from
Section

1.5
. Also recall the definition of the Hilbert–Schmidt operator-valued map
R(\cdot\|\cdot)
from (
10
). Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigg\{f\in C^{1}((-1,\infty)):\ f(0)=0,\ xf^{\prime}(x)>0\text{ for }x\not=0,

(17a)

\displaystyle\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}f(x)=\infty,\ f^{\prime}\text{ Lipschitz at }0\bigg\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}\ni(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{1})\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{f}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\coloneqq\sum_{i}f(\Lambda_{i}(R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))):\ f\in\mathscr{F}\right\}.

(17b)

As we show in
Lemma

4.1
below, the conditions
f\in C^{1}((-1,\infty))
and
xf^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x\not=0
ensure that
0
is the unique minimiser of every
f\in\mathscr{F}
. The Lipschitz continuity of
f^{\prime}
at
0
implies that
(f(x_{i}))_{i}
is summable for
(x_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
, so that every
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
satisfies
\mathcal{L}(\cdot\|\cdot)<\infty
. Furthermore, this Lipschitz continuity implies that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\cdot)
is differentiable on a suitable subspace of
\mathcal{E}
, as will be shown later in
Lemma

4.13
. The blowup at infinity condition is used to prove coercivity of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\cdot)
on suitable subspaces of
\mathcal{E}
, as we show in
Lemma

4.21
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be given in (
17a
) and
f\in\mathscr{F}
. Then

(i)

f^{\prime}(x)=0
if and only if
x=0
, the image of
f
lies in
[0,\infty)
and for every
x\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
it holds that
\sum_{i}f(x_{i})<\infty
. In particular, the image of every
\mathcal{L}_{f}\in\mathscr{L}
lies in
[0,\infty)
.

(ii)

Let
\eta:(-1,\infty)\rightarrow(-1,\infty)
be defined by
\eta(x)=\frac{-x}{1+x}
. If
f\in\mathscr{F}
and
\lim_{x\rightarrow-1}f(x)=\infty
, then
f\circ\eta\in\mathscr{F}
.

Remark 4.2

(Choice of spectral loss functions)
.

The class of loss functions considered in the finite-dimensional setting of
[
35
, Definition 2.1]
differs from the class (
17
) in two aspects. First, for every function
f
in the former, the domain is
(0,\infty)
and
f
need not have minimum equal to 0,
while for every function in the latter, the domain is
(-1,\infty)
and we require
f(0)=0
. To motivate this difference with
[
35
, Definition 2.1]
, we note that the fundamental object governing the losses is given by the operator
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})
defined in (
10
). This operator is compact and therefore has an eigenvalue sequence accumulating at 0. The horizontal shift of
-1
and the vertical shift, which together ensure that
f
has minimum
0
at
x=0
, are therefore natural. Second, there is an additional Lipschitz condition in (
17a
), which implies that
\mathcal{L}(\cdot\|\cdot)<\infty
on
\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}
for every
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
.

Note that the condition that
f^{\prime}
is Lipschitz continuous at
0
is not implied by the other conditions in (
17a
), as the function
f
with
f^{\prime}(x)=\operatorname{sgn}(x)\lvert x\rvert^{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
f(0)=0
shows. Here,
\operatorname{sgn}(x)
denotes the function assigning
1
to
x\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise. This function satisfies all conditions of (
17a
) except the Lipschitz condition of
f^{\prime}
at 0.

While restricted compared to the class in
[
35
, Definition 2.1]
, the class (
17
) is still rich enough to include the forward and reverse KL divergences and Rényi divergences between equivalent Gaussian measures with the same mean, as shown in the following result. This result partially extends
[
35
, Lemma 2.2]
, in which the analogous statement is shown for the forward KL divergence in the finite-dimensional setting.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
m\in\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mu_{i}=\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C}_{i})
be nondegenerate and
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}
for
i=1,2
.

(i)

Let
f_{\textup{KL}}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(x-\log(1+x))
. Then
f_{\textup{KL}}\in\mathscr{F}
and

\displaystyle D_{\textup{KL}}(\mu_{2}\|\mu_{1})=-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\nolimits_{2}(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))=\mathcal{L}_{f_{\textup{KL}}}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}).

(ii)

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
and
f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\rho-1}{2\rho(1-\rho)}\log(1+x)+\frac{1}{2\rho(1-\rho)}\log{(\rho+(1-\rho)(1+x))}.
Then
f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}\in\mathscr{F}
and

\displaystyle D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\mu_{2}\|\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\det\left[\bigl(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\bigr)^{\rho-1}\bigl(\rho I+(1-\rho)(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))\bigr)\right]}{2\rho(1-\rho)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}).

(iii)

For the reverse divergences, we have
f_{\textup{KL}}\circ\eta,f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}\circ\eta\in\mathscr{F}
with
\eta(x)\coloneqq\frac{-x}{1+x}
on
(-1,\infty)
, and

\displaystyle D_{\textup{KL}}(\mu_{1}\|\mu_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{f_{\textup{KL}}\circ\eta}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}),\quad D_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\mu_{1}\|\mu_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}\circ\eta}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}).

Given the approximation classes (
5
) and (
6
) and given the covariance loss functions in (
17b
), we can define the following low-rank approximation problems, for every
r\leq n
. We do not consider the case
r>n
, because in this case the problems have the trivial solutions
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}
respectively.

Problem 4.4

(Rank-
r
nonpositive covariance updates)
.

Find
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}
such that for every
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})=\min\{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}):\ \mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}\}
.

Problem 4.5

(Rank-
r
nonnegative precision updates)
.

Find
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}
such that for every
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})^{-1})=\min\{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{P}^{-1}):\ \mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}\}
.

We note that even if an optimal covariance and precision can be found for some given
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
, it is not a priori clear that they are in fact independent of
\mathcal{L}
.

We also emphasise the following. Since the inverse of a self-adjoint positive matrix is again self-adjoint and positive, inverses of covariance operators are covariance operators in the case
\dim\mathcal{H}<\infty
, but not if
\dim\mathcal{H}=\infty
. This is because the trace-class property is not preserved under inversion in infinite dimensions. In fact, if
\dim{\mathcal{H}}=\infty
and
T
is trace class, then
T^{-1}
is an unbounded operator and its eigenvalue sequence is not summable since this sequence is not bounded. In order to define a loss on precisions, as is done in the finite-dimensional case of
[
35
, Corollary 3.1]
, one approach is to extend the domain of
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
from
(\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E})
to
(\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E})\cup(\mathcal{E}^{-1}\times\mathcal{E}^{-1})
where
\mathcal{E}^{-1}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}^{-1}:\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}\}
. This can be done by setting
\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1})\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})
and
\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})
for
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
.
If
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})\in(\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E})\cap(\mathcal{E}^{-1}\times\mathcal{E}^{-1})
, then
\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})
, showing that
\mathcal{L}_{ext}
is well-defined.
By the definitions (
17
), (
10
) and
Lemma

3.4
,
\mathcal{L}
depends on
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
only via the set of eigenvalues of the bounded extensions of the densely defined operator
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}-I
.
Lemma

3.4
(i)
-
(ii)
show that eigenvalues of the latter operator remain unchanged when replacing
\mathcal{C}_{1}
by
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
by
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}
. Thus,
\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1})=\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{1}\|\mathcal{C}_{2})
. This equation firstly implies that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{P}^{-1})=\mathcal{L}_{ext}(\mathcal{P}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1})
for
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}
, and we can reformulate
Problem

4.5
accordingly in terms of a loss
\mathcal{L}_{ext}
on precisions. Secondly, it shows that there is no need to explicitly define a loss on precisions, as we can just use
\mathcal{L}
on the corresponding covariances in reverse order instead.

4.2
Equivalence to target measures of low-rank Gaussian approximations

As discussed in
Section

3
, not all approximations
\mathcal{N}(m_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*})
are probability measures equivalent to
\mu_{\textup{pos}}
. This equivalence holds only if
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathcal{E}
, with
\mathcal{E}
defined in (
7
). The first aim of this section is to characterise the sets
\mathscr{C}_{r,\mathcal{E}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathcal{E}:\ K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r,\mathcal{E}}\coloneqq\{(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*})^{-1}:\ \mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathcal{E},\ K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\}
, which is done in
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
and
Proposition

4.7
(i)
respectively.
We also show that
\mathscr{C}_{r}\subset\mathscr{C}_{r,\mathcal{E}}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}\subset\mathscr{P}_{r,\mathcal{E}}
, with
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
from (
5
) and (
6
) respectively, and that these inclusions are strict. In this section, we also determine the relationship between
\mathscr{C}_{r}^{-1}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}^{-1}:\ \mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}\}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
, and between
Problem

4.4
and
Problem

4.5
.

We shall characterise the elements in
\mathcal{E}
of the form
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}
with
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
using
Lemma

4.6
. Because this result is not intrinsic to the Bayesian interpretation, we formulate it for the more general set
\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
defined in (
8
), which contains all covariances
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C})\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
, for arbitrary Gaussian target measures
\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
with
m_{1}\in\mathcal{H}
and injective
\mathcal{C}_{1}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Lemma

4.6
shows that the operator
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is important for determining whether
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
belongs to
\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
.
Item

(ii)
shows that the assumption that
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is well-defined and nonnegative, is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}\geq 0
, which is necessary for
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
.
Item

(iii)
shows that this assumption with the additional assumption of invertibility of
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is both necessary and sufficient for
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
. By
item

(i)
,
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is well-defined under the range condition
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
.
Item

(iv)
relates the properties of the eigenvalues of
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
to the properties
\mathcal{C}\geq 0
or
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
of
\mathcal{C}
. If
\mathcal{C}>0
, then
item

(iv)
together with
Lemma

A.1
also shows that the range condition
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
implies the diagonalisability of
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
in the Cameron–Martin space of
\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
be injective and
m_{1}\in\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
for some
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
and
