r\in\mathbb{N}
. The following holds:

(i)

If
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
, then
\mathfrak{X}\coloneqq I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is well-defined and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
,

(ii)

\mathcal{C}\geq 0
if and only if
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and
\mathfrak{X}\geq 0
,

(iii)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
, with
\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
defined in (
8
),

(b)

\mathcal{C}>0
and
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{1/2}
,

(c)

\mathcal{C}\geq 0
and
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{1/2}
,

(d)

\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
,
\mathfrak{X}\geq 0
and
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{1/2}
,

(e)

\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
,
\mathfrak{X}\geq 0
and
\mathfrak{X}
is invertible.

(iv)

Let
\mathcal{C}\geq 0
. Then
\mathfrak{X}=I-\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}d_{i}^{2}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
with
(d_{i}^{2})_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\subset(0,1]
nonincreasing and
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}
orthonormal. The equivalent statements of
item

(iii)
hold if and only if
(d_{i}^{2})_{i}\subset(0,1)
. If additionally to
\mathcal{C}\geq 0
also
\mathcal{C}>0
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
holds, then
(d_{i}^{2})_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\subset(0,1)
and
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{1/2}
.

With
Lemma

4.6
describing those elements in
\mathcal{E}
of the form
\mathcal{C_{\textup{pr}}}-KK^{*}
with
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
, we can now characterise the inverses of these elements, and also the inverses of the elements in
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
. As we did for
Lemma

4.6
, we state the result for low-rank approximations of injective covariances of arbitrary Gaussian measures, rather than only for the prior.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{1}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
,
m_{1}\in\mathcal{H}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is injective. The following hold:

(i)

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
for
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is injective and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}
for some
Z\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. In this case,
\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
.

(ii)

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
for
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is injective,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}+UU^{*}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
for some
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. In this case,
\operatorname{rank}\left({U}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
.

(iii)

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
for
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{C}>0
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is injective,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}+UU^{*}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
for some
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. In this case,
\operatorname{rank}\left({U}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
.

Item

(iii)
is a slight reformulation of
item

(ii)
, and is useful in view of the definition (
5
) and (
6
), because with
(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\leftarrow(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
it shows that
\mathscr{C}_{r}^{-1}=\mathscr{P}_{r}
. This fact is summarised in
Corollary

4.10
(i)
. Furthermore, a comparison of
item

(ii)
and
item

(iii)
shows that for
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}
with
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
, we have the equivalent statements

(i)

\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}>0
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
, and

(ii)

\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathcal{E}
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
.

Hence,
\mathscr{C}_{r}\subset\mathcal{E}
. This fact is reiterated in
Corollary

4.11
.

We comment on the difference between the statements in
items

(i)
and
(ii)
. If the equivalent conditions of
item

(i)
hold, then
item

(i)
implies
(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}h\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
for any
h\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}
. However, this does not imply that
k_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}h
and
k_{2}\coloneqq ZZ^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}h
lie in
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
, only that their sum
k_{1}+k_{2}
does. Under the additional condition that
k_{1},k_{2}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
, we may write
\mathcal{C}^{-1}h=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}k_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}k_{2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}h+\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}ZZ^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}h
. We can formulate the latter as
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}h+UU^{*}h
for a suitable
U
, as shown in the proof.
Thus, to be able to write
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}+\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}ZZ^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on all of
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}
, as in the formulation of
item

(ii)
, one needs to impose restrictions on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}
, and hence on
\operatorname{ran}{K}
, in
item

(i)
. As
item

(ii)
shows, the required condition is precisely
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
.

We give an example of
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
with
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
for which
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
but not
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
, which shows that the additional condition
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
in
item

(ii)
compared to
item

(i)
is not vacuous.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be infinite-dimensional, so that
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
is a proper subspace of
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
. Let
h\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}\backslash\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
and define
k\coloneqq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}h\rVert^{-1}h
so that
z\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}k
has unit norm. With
\varphi
any unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
, we define the rank-1 operator
K\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}k\otimes\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. Hence
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-\frac{1}{4}k\otimes k
satisfies
\operatorname{ran}{K}=\operatorname{span}{\left(k\right)}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\not\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
. Furthermore,
I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}=I-\frac{1}{4}z\otimes z
is nonnegative and invertible by
Lemma

A.9
applied with
e_{1}\leftarrow z
,
\delta_{1}\leftarrow-\frac{1}{4}
and
\delta_{i}\leftarrow 0
for
i>1
. By the implication
(iii)(e)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(a)
in
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
, which furnishes the desired example.

In our Bayesian context, i.e. setting
(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\leftarrow(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
,
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
shows that for all
\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
which satisfy
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}
for some
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
, it holds that
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
. However, as
Proposition

4.7
(i)
-
(ii)
shows, not all
\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
which satisfy
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}
for some
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
have associated precision of the form (
6
), only those for which not only
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
but also
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
holds. In general, the precisions of covariance operators
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}
of the form
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}
are of the form
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}
. Of course, if
\dim{\mathcal{H}}<\infty
, then
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}=\mathcal{H}
and both forms always agree, so that the difference between
Proposition

4.7
(i)
-
(ii)
disappears.

Since
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{obs}}+G\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}G^{*})^{-1/2}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}
, the update (
4b
) of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
and
Proposition

4.7
(ii)
or
Proposition

4.7
(iii)
with
r\leftarrow n
and
(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\leftarrow(0,\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
show that
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}}
. This provides another argument showing that
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}}
, besides the explicit computation of
[
36
, Example 6.23]
.

Remark 4.8

(Choice of approximation class)
.

A natural generalisation to infinite dimensions of the low-rank approximation classes for covariance and precision considered in the finite-dimensional setting of
[
35
, eqs. (2.4) and (4.1)]
, is to take
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}_{r}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}>0:\ K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}=\{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}:\ U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\}
. Let
\mathscr{C}_{r,\mathcal{E}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathcal{E}:\ K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\}
as in the start of
Section

4.2
. By the preceding discussion, we have the proper inclusion
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}^{-1}\subset\mathscr{C}_{r,\mathcal{E}}
, and by the equivalence
(iii)(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)(a)
of
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
also the proper inclusion
\mathscr{C}_{r,\mathcal{E}}\subset\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
holds. That is, in general
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}^{-1}
contains strictly fewer covariances than those that maintain equivalence between the resulting approximate and exact posterior, while
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
contains strictly more. The loss classes considered in this work require densities to exist, thus the set
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
is not suitable. Note that by the definition of
\mathscr{P}_{r}
in (
6
),
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}=\mathscr{P}_{r}
. The fact that
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}^{-1}\subset\mathscr{C}_{r,\mathcal{E}}
motivates the use of approximation class
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}
in this work. Because the form of the precision updates in
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{r}
parallels the form in
[
35
, eq. (4.1)]
that lies at the core of the approximation procedure of
[
35
]
, our work can be considered to naturally generalise
[
35
]
.

Example 4.9
.

To illustrate the conditions of
Lemma

4.6
and
Proposition

4.7
in an example, we consider the setting of
[
36
, Example 6.16]
.
Consider the infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}\coloneqq L^{2}((0,1))
and let
H^{1}_{0}((0,1))
denote the Sobolev space of functions in
\mathcal{H}
which vanish on the boundary of
(0,1)
and have a square-integrable weak derivative. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we also denote by
H^{m}((0,1))
the Sobolev space of functions in
\mathcal{H}
with
m
square-integrable weak derivatives. We can then define the one-dimensional Laplacian
{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}:H^{1}_{0}((0,1))\cap H^{2}((0,1))\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, which is an unbounded operator with nonpositive spectrum. Its eigenpairs are given by
(a_{i},h_{i})\coloneqq(i^{2}\pi^{2},\sqrt{2}\sin(i\pi x))_{i}
and
(h_{i})_{i}
forms an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
.
Consider the Gaussian measure
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{1})
on
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathcal{C}_{1}\coloneqq(-{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}}^{2}})^{-m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The Cameron–Martin space
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
is given by
H^{1}_{0}((0,1))\cap H^{m}((0,1))
. By
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
(iii)(a)
-
(iii)(e)
, it follows that a finite-rank update
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
with
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
yields a Gaussian measure
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C})
that is equivalent to
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{1})
if and only if
K
maps into
H^{1}_{0}((0,1))\cap H^{m}((0,1))
and

\mathfrak{X}\coloneqq I-([-{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}]^{m/2}K)([-{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}]^{m/2}K)^{*}
is nonnegative and invertible. For example, if
m=1
,
r=1
, and
Kt\coloneqq cth
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, for some
h\in\mathcal{H}
and scaling parameter
c>0
, then we require that
h\in H^{1}_{0}((0,1))
and that
\mathfrak{X}=I-c^{2}([{\operatorname{d}\!}/{\operatorname{d}\!{{\mathrm{x}}}}]^{1/2}h)\otimes([{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}}]^{1/2}h)
is nonnegative and invertible. The choice
h\coloneqq h_{1}\in H^{1}_{0}((0,1))
implies
[{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}}]^{1/2}h=\pi h
, so that
\mathfrak{X}=I-c^{2}\pi^{2}P
with
P\coloneqq h_{1}\otimes h_{1}
the orthogonal projector in
\mathcal{H}
onto
\operatorname{span}{\left(h_{1}\right)}
. Thus, this choice yields an equivalent measure for any
c<1/\pi
. Since
h_{1}\in H^{1}_{0}((0,1))\cap H^{2}((0,1))=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
as well,
Proposition

4.7
(ii)
implies that
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}^{2}}}+k\otimes k
on
H^{1}_{0}((0,1))\cap H^{2}((0,1))
for some
k\in\mathcal{H}
.
The condition
h\in H^{1}_{0}((0,1))
is violated for functions
h
that do not vanish on the boundary of
(0,1)
, or functions
h
that lack sufficient smoothness, such as indicator functions.

Corollary

4.10
(i)
below shows that
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
are in one-to-one correspondence by the operation of taking inverses, and can be seen as a generalisation of the finite-dimensional result
[
35
, Lemma A.2]
to infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.
Corollary

4.10
(ii)
shows that one may solve
Problem

4.4
by solving
Problem

4.5
and vice versa.

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}
be as in (
5
) and (
6
) respectively.

(i)

For every
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}
, there exists
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
of the same rank as
K
, such that
(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*})^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}
. The reverse correspondence also holds: for every
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}
, there exists
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
of the same rank as
U
, such that
(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-KK^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}
. In particular,
\mathscr{C}_{r}^{-1}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}^{-1}:\ \mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}\}=\mathscr{P}_{r}
and
\mathscr{P}_{r}^{-1}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}^{-1}:\ \mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}\}=\mathscr{C}_{r}
.

(ii)

An approximation
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}
solves
Problem

4.4
if and only if
(\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})^{-1}\in\mathscr{P}_{r}
solves
Problem

4.5
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\textup{opt}})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{P}_{r}^{\textup{opt}})^{-1})
.

As discussed after
Proposition

4.7
it holds that
\mathscr{C}_{r}\subset\mathcal{E}
. Hence
Problem

4.4
is well-defined in the sense that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\cdot)
is finite on
\mathscr{C}_{r}
for any
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
. By
Corollary

4.10
(ii)
, it follows that
Problem

4.5
is analogously well-defined. These facts are emphasised below.

Corollary 4.11
.

It holds that
\mathscr{C}_{r}\subset\mathcal{E}
. Thus, for any
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
, the map
\mathcal{C}\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C})
is finite on
\mathscr{C}_{r}
and the map
\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{P}^{-1})
is finite on
\mathscr{P}_{r}
.

4.3
Differentiability and minimisers of covariance loss function

In order to solve
Problem

4.5
, we formulate it as a minimisation problem over the set of
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. By
Corollary

4.10
(ii)
, solving
Problem

4.4
is equivalent to solving
Problem

4.5
.
We want to find the minimiser of the function

J_{f}:\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad U\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{f}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1}),

(18)

for any
f\in\mathscr{F}
and
\mathcal{L}_{f}\in\mathscr{L}
defined in (
17
), which we shall express as a composition of functions. This composition will facilitate the analysis of its differentiability and thereby the identification of its stationary points.

As described in
Section

1.5
, we denote by
\Lambda
an eigenvalue map defined on
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Fix an arbitrary
f\in\mathscr{F}
. The restriction of
\Lambda
to the self-adjoint Hilbert–Schmidt operators with eigenvalues sequence in
(-1,\infty)
shall be postcomposed with the functions

\displaystyle F_{f}:\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))\rightarrow[0,\infty),\quad F_{f}((x_{i})_{i})=\sum_{i}f(x_{i}),

(19)

which are well-defined by
Lemma

4.1
(i)
. If
P\in\mathcal{B}(\ell_{2}((-1,\infty)))
is a permutation, so that
((Px)_{i})_{i}=(x_{\pi(i)})_{i}
for some bijection
\pi
of
\mathbb{N}
, then
F_{f}(Px)=F_{f}(x)
for any
x\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
.

We finally define the function
g:\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
by

\displaystyle g(U)=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2},

(20)

where
H
is the Hessian given in (
3
).
That is,
g(U)
is a nonnegative, self-adjoint, rank-
r
update of the negative of the posterior-preconditioned Hessian.
The image of
g
in fact consists of Hilbert–Schmidt operators which can be diagonalised in the Cameron–Martin space, as is shown next. This result also motivates the definition of
g
, as it shows that
g(U)
has the same eigenvalues as
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1})
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
and
g
be as in (
20
). Then
\operatorname{rank}\left({g(U)}\right)\leq r+\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
and there exists a sequence
(e_{i})_{i}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
which forms an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
and a sequence
(\gamma_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
satisfying

g(U)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
.
Finally, the eigenvalues of
g(U)
and
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1})
agree, counting multiplicities.

As a consequence of
Lemma

4.12
, we can write
J_{f}
as

\displaystyle J_{f}(U)=F_{f}(\Lambda(R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1})))=F_{f}\circ\Lambda\circ g\ (U),

(21)

which yields the desired reformulation of the loss as a composition of functions. We use
[
4
, Theorem 12.4.5 (i)]
to search for a solution of
Problem

4.5
in the set of stationary points of
J_{f}
. For this, we need to show that
J_{f}
is Gateaux differentiable. To do so, we use the following result, which states that
g
and
F_{f}
are Fréchet differentiable and gives an explicit form of the derivatives. Gateaux- and Fréchet derivatives are infinite-dimensional analogues of directional and total derivatives, see for example
[
19
, Section 3.6]
,
[
18
, Section 1.4]
or
[
4
, Section 12.1]
for the definition of Gateaux and Fréchet differentiability.

Lemma 4.13
.

The functions
g
and
F_{f}
defined in (
20
) and (
19
) respectively are Fréchet differentiable, with derivatives

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(U)(V)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2},

\displaystyle U,V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H}),

\displaystyle F_{f}^{\prime}(x)(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i},

\displaystyle x\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty)),\ y\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Remark 4.14

(Necessity of assumptions on
\mathscr{F}
)
.

For a finite set of indices
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
, the convergence
(f(x_{i}+y_{i})-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i})/y_{i}\rightarrow 0
is uniform in
i
. This implies that
\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}(\sum_{i}^{l}f(x_{i}+y_{i})-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i})\rightarrow 0
, which implies differentiability of
F_{f}
for finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}
. In infinite dimensions, the convergence of each term is no longer uniform in
i
, as now
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and the previous sum need not converge to 0. Compared to a finite-dimensional setting, in the infinite-dimensional setting we therefore need more assumptions on
f
to obtain the desired convergence. Hence we restrict the function
f
to the class of spectral functions
\mathscr{F}
from (
17a
). In particular, we require additionally that
f
has minimum 0 and a derivative which is Lipschitz at 0.

Let
U,V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
.
If
\mathcal{W}\subset L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
is a subspace of finite dimension that contains
g(U+tV)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then the restriction
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}:\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of
F_{f}\circ\Lambda
to
\mathcal{W}
satisfies
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}\circ g(U+tV)=F_{f}\circ\Lambda\circ g(U+tV)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
F_{f}\circ\Lambda\circ g
is Gateaux differentiable at
U
in the direction
V
if and only if
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}\circ g
is. Hence, by the chain rule, e.g.
[
18
, Section 1.4.1]
or
[
4
, Theorem 12.2.2]
, it suffices to show that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
is Fréchet differentiable on all of
(\mathcal{W},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})})
and that
g
is Gateaux differentiable at
U
in the direction
V
in order to show the Gateaux differentiability of
J_{f}=F_{f}\circ\Lambda\circ g
at
U
in the direction
V
. This observation is useful, since such a finite-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{W}
exists, e.g.
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{X\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H}):\ \operatorname{ran}{X}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}VV^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}
}. This subspace is finite-dimensional because
U
,
V
, and
H
are finite-rank.

We now use the finite-dimensional result of
[
22
, Theorem 1.1]
on differentiability of permutation invariant functions of spectra of symmetric matrices to deduce Fréchet differentiability of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
for certain
\mathcal{W}\subset L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
F:\mathbb{R}^{\dim{\mathcal{H}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. This is done in
Proposition

4.18
, using
Proposition

4.16
. For this purpose, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. A set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is symmetric if
Px\in\Omega
for every
x\in\Omega
and every permutation
P:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is symmetric, then a function
\mathcal{G}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is symmetric if
\mathcal{G}(Px)=\mathcal{G}(x)
for every
x\in\Omega
and every permutation
P:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

As an example of a symmetric set and a symmetric function, consider the set
\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
and the function
F_{f}
from (
19
) for any
f\in\mathscr{F}
.

Recall from
Section

1.5
the definition of the eigenvalue map
\Lambda^{m}:L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
for an
m
-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{H}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The ordering of eigenvalues given by
\Lambda^{m}
is nonincreasing.
The following result relates the Fréchet differentiability of
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
and
\mathcal{G}
for symmetric functions
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let the set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be open and symmetric, and suppose that
\mathcal{G}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is symmetric. Let
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{H}
be
m
-dimensional and let
X\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
be such that
\Lambda^{m}(X)\in\Omega
.
Then the function
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}:L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is Fréchet differentiable at
X
if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Lambda^{m}(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. In this case the Fréchet derivative of
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
at
X
is

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(X)=\sum_{i}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z}),

where
(e_{i})_{i}
is an orthonormal sequence in
\mathcal{Z}
satisfying
X=\sum_{i}(\Lambda^{m}(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
.

Remark 4.17
.

By definition of the Fréchet derivative,
(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(X)\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
. By the Riesz representation theorem,
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}\simeq L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
, and we consider
(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(X)
as an element of
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.

As a consequence of
Proposition

4.16
, which is a result on Fréchet differentiability for symmetric functions of spectra of operators in
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})
for
\dim{\mathcal{Z}}<\infty
, we can now deduce the Fréchet differentiability of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
for symmetric functions
F
and suitable finite-dimensional subspaces
\mathcal{W}
of
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional subspace. Let
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{X\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}:\ \operatorname{ran}{X}\subset\mathcal{Z}\}\subset L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
F:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric function and let
X\in\mathcal{W}
.
Then
\ker{X}^{\perp}=\operatorname{ran}{X}
, and if
F
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Lambda(X)
, then
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}:\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is Fréchet differentiable at
X\in\mathcal{W}
. In this case, the Fréchet derivative is given by

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\prime}(X)=\sum_{i}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}},

where
(e_{i})_{i}
is an orthonormal sequence in
\mathcal{Z}
satisfying
X=\sum_{i}\Lambda(X)_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}.

Remark 4.19
.

The stated differentiability of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}:\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
holds with respect to the subspace topology on
\mathcal{W}
inherited from
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. That is, we consider
\mathcal{W}
as a Hilbert space with its
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}
norm.

Recall that
J_{f}=F_{f}\circ\Lambda\circ g
by (
21
). We can now prove Gateaux differentiability of
J_{f}
.

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
F_{f}
,
g
, and
J_{f}
be as defined in (
19
), (
20
), and (
21
) respectively. Then
J_{f}
is Gateaux differentiable on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
, and for any
U,V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
, the Gateaux derivative at
U
in the direction
V
is given by

\displaystyle J_{f}^{\prime}(U)(V)=2\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\Lambda_{i}(g(U)))\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},VU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle,

where
(e_{i})_{i}
is an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
satisfying
g(U)=\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i}(g(U))e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
.

A coercive and Gateaux differentiable function has a global minimum, that can be found among its stationary points. The following lemma establishes the coercivity of
J_{f}
over every finite-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
, that is, it establishes the coercivity of
J_{f}
over
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
for every finite-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{F}
and
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{H}
be finite-dimensional. Then
J_{f}
is coercive over
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
, i.e.
J_{f}(U_{n})\rightarrow\infty
whenever
\lVert U_{n}\rVert\rightarrow\infty
. In particular,
J_{f}
has a global minimum on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
, which can be found among the stationary points of the restriction of
J_{f}
to
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}
).

Unless
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional, the function
J_{f}
is not coercive on all of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. To show this, we exploit the property that the finite-dimensional ranges of a sequence
U_{n}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
do not need to lie in the same finite-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}
.

Example 4.22
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
r=1
. Furthermore, let
(e_{k})_{k}
be the standard basis of
\mathcal{H}
and put
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}e_{k}=k^{-\alpha}e_{k}
for
\alpha>1
. Let
U_{m}t=m^{\beta}te_{m}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\beta>0
. Then
U_{m}U_{m}^{*}e_{k}=\delta_{m,k}m^{2\beta}e_{m}
. It follows that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{m}U_{m}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{k}=\delta_{m,k}m^{-\alpha+2\beta}e_{m}
. Hence
\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{m}U_{m}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert^{2}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{m}U_{m}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{m},e_{m}\rangle=m^{-\alpha+2\beta}
which is bounded from above for
\alpha>2\beta
. Therefore, for
\alpha>2\beta
,
\lVert g(U_{m})\rVert
is bounded in
m
by the triangle inequality, while
\lVert U_{m}\rVert=m^{\beta}\rightarrow\infty
. We now argue that
J_{f}(U_{m})
is bounded in
m
.
Let
\gamma_{m}
be the eigenvalue of largest magnitude among the eigenvalues of
g(U_{m})
.
Lemma

A.3
implies
\gamma_{m}=\lVert g(U_{m})\rVert
is bounded in
m
for
\alpha>2\beta
.
Now,
f(\gamma)\leq f(\gamma_{m})+f(-\gamma_{m})
for every eigenvalue
\gamma
of
g(U_{m})
, because
xf^{\prime}(x)<0
for
x\not=0
implies that
f
increases as
\lvert x\rvert
increases. By
Lemma

4.12
, at most
n+r
eigenvalues of
g(U_{m})
are nonzero. Because
f(0)=0
for
f\in\mathscr{F}
in (
17a
), we conclude from (
21
), (
19
) and continuity of
f
that
J_{f}(U_{m})\leq(n+r)(f(\gamma_{m})+f(-\gamma_{m}))
is bounded in
m
.

In the proof of Theorem
4.23
, coercivity on finite-dimensional subspaces of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
of the form
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
for finite-dimensional
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{H}
is enough to show the existence of a global minimiser of
J_{f}
, because all the stationary points lie in one such finite-dimensional subspace.

4.4
Optimal low-rank posterior covariance approximations

We can now state the solutions to
Problem

4.4
and
Problem

4.5
. In these solutions, the eigenpairs
(\lambda_{i},w_{i})_{i}
of the operator
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
defined in (
10
), occurring in the Feldman–Hajek theorem relating the prior and the posterior distributions, take a central role.

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
r\leq n
and let
(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,0])
and
(w_{i})_{i}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
be as given in
Proposition

3.7
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i})\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}),

(22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}-\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i})\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}).

(23)

Then
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
are solutions to
Problem

4.5
and
Problem

4.4
respectively and
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
are inverses of each other. For every
f\in\mathscr{F}
, the associated minimal loss is
\mathcal{L}_{f}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})=\sum_{i>r}f(\lambda_{i})
. The solutions
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
are unique if and only if the following holds:
\lambda_{r+1}=0
or
\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r+1}
.

Remark 4.24

(Uniqueness condition)
.

Two remarks are in order when comparing the uniqueness characterisations of
Theorem

4.23
and of its finite-dimensional analogue in
[
35
, Theorem 2.3 and Corollary 3.1]
. Firstly, the condition in
Theorem

4.23
is not only sufficient but also necessary. On the contrary,
[
35
]
only states a sufficient condition. Secondly, the sufficient condition of
[
35
, Theorem 2.3 and Corollary 3.1]
is that
(\frac{-\lambda_{1}}{1+\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\frac{-\lambda_{r}}{1+\lambda_{r}})
are different, i.e. that
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
are different. From
Theorem

4.23
, we see that this condition should be interpreted as the condition that
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
are different from
\lambda_{r+1}
or
\lambda_{r+1}=0
. Indeed, if
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
are different among each other but
\lambda_{r}=\lambda_{r+1}\not=0
, then replacing
(\lambda_{r},w_{r})
by
(\lambda_{r+1},w_{r+1})
in (
23
) and (
22
) gives a different solution to
Problem

4.4
and
Problem

4.5
respectively.

Example 4.25
.

We consider simultaneously diagonalisable prior and posterior covariance operators in the setting
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}((0,1))
of
Example

4.9
, based on
[
36
, Example 6.17]
. Suppose that the prior and the posterior covariance are given by
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}=(-{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}}^{2}})^{-1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}=(-{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}/{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}+I)^{-1}
respectively.
The prior and the posterior constructed in this way indeed form equivalent measures, see
[
36
, Example 6.17]
.
The eigenpairs of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
are given by
(a_{i}^{-1},w_{i})_{i}
and of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
by
((1+a_{i})^{-1},w_{i})_{i}
, with

\displaystyle a_{i}\coloneqq i^{2}\pi^{2},\quad\text{and}\quad w_{i}\coloneqq\sqrt{2}\sin(i\pi x).

Then,
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
is diagonalisable in the same ONB
(w_{i})_{i}
, with eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}=a_{i}/(1+a_{i})-1=-1/(1+a_{i})
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}=\sqrt{a_{i}}w_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}=\sqrt{a_{i}}^{-1}w_{i}
, and
-\lambda_{i}/(1+\lambda_{i})=1/a_{i}
the optimal rank-
r
posterior precision and covariance approximations are now given by
Theorem

4.23
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}^{2}}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}w_{i}\otimes w_{i},\quad\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}=\left(I-\frac{\operatorname{d}\!{}^{2}}{\operatorname{d}\!{\mathrm{x}^{2}}}\right)^{-1}-\frac{1}{a_{i}(1+a_{i})}\sum_{i=1}^{r}w_{i}\otimes w_{i}.

Theorem

4.23
shows that
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
are the optimal rank-
r
updates of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}
respectively, for all
\mathcal{L}\in\mathscr{L}
simultaneously. By
Lemma

4.3
, this optimality includes optimality with respect to the forward and reverse KL divergences and the Rényi divergences when keeping the mean fixed.
This also holds for the Amari
\alpha
-divergences and the Hellinger distance, by
Remarks

3.9
and
3.10
.
The associated losses can be directly calculated using
Theorem

4.23
. For the Amari
\alpha
-divergences
D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\cdot\|\cdot)
, this follows by (
15
),
Lemma

4.3
(iii)
and the skew symmetry of the Rényi divergences. For the Hellinger distance
D_{\textup{H}}(\cdot,\cdot)
, this follows from (
16
). We summarise these facts in the following corollary.

Corollary 4.26
.

Let
r\leq n
, let
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
be given by (
23
) and
(\lambda_{i})_{i}
as in
Proposition

3.7
. For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
m\in\mathcal{H}
arbitrary, we have

\displaystyle D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\mu_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}))

\displaystyle=\min\{D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\mu_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C})):\ \mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\left(\exp\left(-\alpha(1-\alpha)\sum_{i>r}f_{\textup{Ren},\alpha}\left(\lambda_{i}\right)\right)-1\right),

\displaystyle D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})\|\mu_{\textup{pos}})

\displaystyle=\min\{D_{\textup{Am},\alpha}(\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C})\|\mu_{\textup{pos}})):\ \mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\left(\exp\left(-\alpha(1-\alpha)\sum_{i>r}f_{\textup{Ren},\alpha}\left(\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}\right)\right)-1\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\left(\exp\left(-\alpha(1-\alpha)\sum_{i>r}f_{\textup{Ren},1-\alpha}\left(\lambda_{i}\right)\right)-1\right),

where
f_{\textup{Ren},\alpha}
is given in
Lemma

4.3
(ii)
. Furthermore, for arbitrary
m\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle D_{\textup{H}}(\mu_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}))

\displaystyle=\min\{D_{\textup{H}}(\mu_{\textup{pos}},\mathcal{N}(m,\mathcal{C})):\ \mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{r}\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i>r}f_{\textup{Ren},1/2}\left(\lambda_{i}\right)\right)\right)}.

The minimiser
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
is unique if and only if the following holds:
\lambda_{r+1}=0
or
\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r+1}
.

Together,
Theorem

4.23
and
Corollary

4.26
describe those approximate covariances which retain the most posterior covariance information with respect to several divergences simultaneously. After discretising, this allows one to significantly reduce computational costs, c.f.
[
16
, Table 1]
. Computation of the leading
r
discretised versions of the eigenpairs
(\lambda_{i},w_{i})
in
Theorem

4.23
and
Corollary

4.26
requires diagonalising a discretised version of one of the operators in
Proposition

3.7
, for example
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})
or
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
. This can be done efficiently using matrix-free truncated eigensolvers, such as Krylov methods or randomised singular value decomposition, see
[
7
,
16
,
30
]
. Furthermore, given the optimal approximation on function space, one can study the consistency of the discretised approximation with this infinite-dimensional limit. The optimal approximation given in
Theorem

4.23
, which reduces to that of
[
35
]
in a finite-dimensional setting, is then dimension robust when such consistency is proven. The above results thereby enable both tractable and scalable uncertainty quantification for linear Gaussian inverse problems.

4.5
Comparison with finite-dimensional setting

The optimal low-rank posterior covariance approximation procedure was analysed in
[
35
]
in the finite-dimensional setting. The main differences between the results in
[
35
]
and the infinite-dimensional setting of this work are as follows.

(i)

The choice of spectral loss functions, see
Remark

4.2
.

(ii)

The choice of approximation classes for the posterior covariance, see
Remark

4.8
.

(iii)

The given uniqueness condition, see
Remark

4.24
.

We emphasise that
Theorem

4.23
generalises the results of
[
35
]
to the infinite-dimensional setting. In the infinite-dimensional setting, the intricate connection between optimal posterior covariance approximation and the theory of equivalent Gaussian measures becomes apparent. The analysis provided in this work connects posterior covariance approximation with equivalent Gaussian measure theory, and is inherently discretisation-independent. For consistent discretisation schemes, the optimal approximation of
Theorem

4.23
then is dimension robust, and may be obtained by applying
Theorem

4.23
in the discretised setting, i.e. by applying
[
35
, Theorem 2.3]
to the discretised operators occurring in the discretised inverse problem.

5
Conclusion

Linear Gaussian inverse problems on possibly infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces are an important kind of nonparametric inverse problem. For example, they can be used to approximate nonlinear nonparametric problems using the Laplace approximation. They often serve as the native infinite-dimensional formulation of linear inverse problems before the parameter space
\mathcal{H}
is discretised and they are in this sense ‘discretisation independent’.

Optimal low-rank approximation of the posterior covariance for a class of losses that includes the KL divergence and the Hellinger metric, and optimal low-rank approximation of the posterior mean for the Bayes risk were studied in
[
35
]
. The analysis showed that certain matrix pencils, namely the ones defined by the Hessian and prior covariance and the prior and posterior covariance, form the central objects of study. So far, these results applied to finite-dimensional parameter spaces only.

In this work we have formulated the low-rank posterior covariance approximation problem on possibly infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert spaces. We solved this problem and derived the optimal low-rank approximations to the posterior covariance in
Theorem

4.23
. Equivalent conditions for its uniqueness are also given.
This builds upon the finite-dimensional conclusions of
[
35
, Section 2 and 3]
for posterior covariance approximation.
The resulting posterior approximation, obtained by replacing the covariance with the optimal low-rank approximation and by keeping the mean fixed, is equivalent to the exact posterior distribution, and we have shown exactly which low-rank updates of the prior covariance and precision satisfy this equivalence property in
Lemma

4.6
and
Proposition

4.7
.
Furthermore, the posterior covariance approximations are optimal for a class of losses which includes the forward and reverse KL divergences, the Hellinger metric, the Amari
\alpha
-divergences for
\alpha\in(0,1)
and the Rényi divergences.
Finally, we have shown in
Proposition

3.7
that the operator pencils which proved central in the finite-dimensional analysis, are equivalent representations of the Hilbert–Schmidt operator appearing in the Feldman–Hajek theorem which quantifies similarity of Gaussian measures. For linear Gaussian inverse problems, it is therefore this operator that is central to the approximation of the posterior covariance as a low-rank update of the prior covariance. This observation is consistent with the fact that the Hilbert–Schmidt operator in the Feldman–Hajek theorem quantifies the similarity of the Gaussian prior and exact posterior.

The low-rank approximations constructed in this work provide a basis for showing the consistency of optimal low-rank covariance approximations in discretised versions of linear inverse problems. Furthermore, these approximations may be useful for the development of computationally efficient approximations of certain linear Gaussian problems. Finally, they could be used for the approximation of nonlinear nonparametric inverse problems.

6
