The research of the authors has been partially funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG) Project-ID 318763901 – SFB1294. The authors thank
Youssef Marzouk (Massachusetts Institute of Technology) and Bernhard Stankewitz (University of Potsdam) for helpful discussions, and Thomas Mach (University of Potsdam) for suggestions about the manuscript. The authors thank the referees for their helpful feedback.

Appendix A
Auxiliary results

In this section we collect some auxiliary results on Hilbert spaces and bounded operators, unbounded operators and Gaussian measures.

A.1
Hilbert spaces and bounded operators

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a dense subspace and
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
be an orthonormal sequence in
\mathcal{D}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists a countable sequence
(d_{i})_{i}\subset\mathcal{D}
such that
(d_{i})_{i}
is an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
and
d_{i}=e_{i}
for
i\leq m
.

Proof.

The proof is a slight modification of the argument of
[
14
, Lemma A.2]
. By separability of
\mathcal{H}
there exists a countable and dense sequence
(h_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
. By density of
\mathcal{D}
we can construct a countable sequence
(d_{i}^{\prime})_{i}\subset\mathcal{D}
that is dense in
\mathcal{H}
by taking an element of
\mathcal{D}
from the ball
B(h_{i},1/j)
, for all
i
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Now, we apply Gram–Schmidt to the countable sequence
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m},d_{1}^{\prime},d_{2}^{\prime},\ldots)\subset\mathcal{D}
to obtain a countable orthonormal sequence
(d_{i})_{i}\subset\mathcal{D}
. Since
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
is already orthonormal,
d_{i}=e_{i}
for
i\leq m
. Furthermore,
d_{i}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{span}{\left(d_{j},j\leq m+i\right)}
. It follows that
(d_{i}^{\prime})_{i}\subset\operatorname{span}{\left((d_{i})_{i}\right)}
and since
(d_{i}^{\prime})_{i}
is dense, so is
\operatorname{span}{\left((d_{i})_{i}\right)}
.
∎

Lemma A.2

(
[
10
, Proposition II.2.7]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. If
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, then
\lVert A\rVert=\lVert A^{*}\rVert=\lVert AA^{*}\rVert^{1/2}
.

Lemma A.3

(
[
19
, Theorem 4.2.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
be compact and self-adjoint. Then
\lVert A\rVert=\max\{\lvert\lambda\rvert:\ \lambda\text{ is an eigenvalue of }A\}
.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Then
A>0
if and only if
A\geq 0
and
A
is injective.

Proof.

Assume
A
is positive. If
h\in\ker{A}
, then
\langle Ah,h\rangle=0
, so
h=0
.
Now assume
A
is nonnegative and injective. If
\langle Ah,h\rangle=\lVert A^{1/2}h\rVert^{2}=0
for
h\not=0
, then
h\in\ker{A}^{1/2}\subset\ker{A}
, so
h=0
.
∎

Lemma A.5

(
[
19
, Theorem 4.3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces, and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
be compact. Then
A
is diagonalisable, that is, there exists an ONB
(h_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
and an orthonormal sequence
(k_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{K}
and a nonnegative and nonincreasing sequence
(\sigma_{i})_{i}
such that
A=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}k_{i}\otimes h_{i}
.

Lemma A.6

(
[
10
, Proposition VI.1.8]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. Then
\ker{A}=\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}}^{\perp}
and
\ker{A}^{\perp}=\overline{\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}}}
.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. Then
\ker{AA^{*}}=\ker{A^{*}}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\ker{A^{*}}\subset\ker{AA^{*}}
is immediate. If
AA^{*}k=0
for
k\in\mathcal{K}
, then
\lVert A^{*}k\rVert^{2}=\langle AA^{*}k,k\rangle=0
. Hence
A^{*}k=0
, showing the reverse inclusion holds.
∎

Lemma A.8
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces and
A\in\mathcal{B}_{00}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. Then
\operatorname{ran}{AA^{*}}=\operatorname{ran}{A}
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}}
is closed, we have by
Lemma

A.6
that
\operatorname{ran}{AA^{*}}=\operatorname{ran}{AP_{\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}}}}=\operatorname{ran}{AP_{\overline{\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}}}}}=\operatorname{ran}{AP_{\ker{A}^{\perp}}}=\operatorname{ran}{A}
, where
P_{V}
denotes the projection onto a closed subspace
V
of
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space,
(e_{i})_{i}
an orthonormal sequence,
(\delta_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
T\coloneqq I+\sum_{i}\delta_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
. The following holds.

(i)

T
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
if and only if
\delta_{i}\not=-1
for all
i
.

(ii)

T\geq 0
if and only if
\delta_{i}\geq-1
for all
i
.

(iii)

T>0
if and only if
\delta_{i}>-1
for all
i
.

In cases
(i)
and
(iii)
above, the inverse of
T
is
I-\sum_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
T
is invertible. Then
(1+\delta_{i})e_{i}=Te_{i}\not=0
for all
i
, hence
\delta_{i}\not=-1
for all
i
. Conversely, suppose that
\delta_{i}\not=-1
for all
i
and let
k\in\mathcal{H}
. Since
(\delta_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\lvert(1+\delta_{i})^{-1}\rvert\leq 2
for all
i
large enough. Because
(\langle k,e_{i}\rangle)_{i}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, this implies that
\alpha\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
where
\alpha_{i}\coloneqq(1+\delta_{i})^{-1}\langle k,e_{i}\rangle
for all
i
. Hence
h\coloneqq\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
and
Th=\sum_{i}(1+\delta_{i})\langle h,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}=\sum_{i}\langle k,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}=k
. This shows that
T
is surjective. Since
T=T^{*}
,
\ker{T}=\operatorname{ran}{T}^{\perp}=\{0\}
by
Lemma

A.6
, showing that
T
is injective, which proves
(i)
.
If
T\geq 0
, then
1+\delta_{i}=\langle Te_{i},e_{i}\rangle\geq 0
, i.e.
\delta_{i}\geq-1
, for all
i
. Conversely, if
\delta_{i}\geq-1
for all
i
, then
\langle Th,h\rangle=\sum_{i}(1+\delta_{i})\langle h,e_{i}\rangle^{2}\geq 0
. This proves
(ii)
, and replacing “
>
” by “
\geq
”, also
(iii)
.

To compute the inverse of
T
, note that
\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}\leq 2\delta_{i}
for all
i
large enough, by the hypothesis that
(\delta_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus,
\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}\rightarrow 0
and
\sum_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
is well-defined in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. For
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we have by direct computation,

\displaystyle\left(I+\sum_{i}\delta_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)\left(I-\sum_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)h

\displaystyle=\left(I+\sum_{i}\delta_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)\sum_{i}\left(1-\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}\right)\langle h,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left(1-\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}+\delta_{i}-\frac{\delta_{i}^{2}}{1+\delta_{i}}\right)\langle h,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle h,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}=h.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\left(I-\sum_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)\left(I+\sum_{i}\delta_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)h=

\displaystyle\left(I-\sum_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)\sum_{i}\left(1+\delta_{i}\right)\langle h,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left(1+\delta_{i}-\frac{\delta_{i}}{1+\delta_{i}}(1+\delta_{i})\right)\langle h,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}.

∎

Lemma A.10
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces.
Suppose
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{A_{2}}
,

(ii)

There exists
C>0
such that
\{A_{1}h:\ \lVert h\rVert\leq 1\}\subset\{A_{2}h:\ \lVert h\rVert\leq C\}
,

(iii)

There exists
C>0
such that
\lVert A_{1}^{*}k\rVert\leq C\lVert A_{2}^{*}k\rVert
for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

See
[
13
, Proposition B.1(i)]
and its proof.
∎

Definition A.11

(
[
10
, Definition VIII.3.10]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. We say that
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a ‘partial isometry’ if
W
is an isometry on
\ker{W}^{\perp}
. We call
\ker{W}^{\perp}
the ‘initial space’ of
W
and
\operatorname{ran}{W}
the ‘final space’ of
W
.

Recall from
Section

1.5
that
\lvert A\rvert\coloneqq(A^{*}A)^{1/2}
for
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. For a proof of the following, see e.g.
[
10
, VIII.3.11]
.

Lemma A.12

(Polar decomposition)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. There exists a partial isometry
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
with initial space
\ker{A}^{\perp}
and final space
\ker{A^{*}}^{\perp}
such that
A=W\lvert A\rvert
.

Lemma A.13
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
A,B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
be injective with
\operatorname{ran}{AA^{*}}
dense. If
AA^{*}=BB^{*}
, then there exists a Hilbert space isomorphism
Q\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
such that
B=AQ
.

Proof.

We first note that
\operatorname{ran}{A}
is dense, since
\operatorname{ran}{AA^{*}}=A(\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}})\subset A(\overline{\operatorname{ran}{A^{*}}})=A(\ker{A}^{\perp})=A(\mathcal{H})=\operatorname{ran}{A}
by
Lemma

A.6
and
\ker{A}=\{0\}
. Since
AA^{*}=BB^{*}
, also
\operatorname{ran}{BB^{*}}
and
\operatorname{ran}{B}
are dense.
Now, by the polar decomposition applied to
A^{*}
and
B^{*}
, c.f.
Lemma

A.12
, there exist
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
such that
A^{*}=W_{1}\lvert A^{*}\rvert
,
B^{*}=W_{2}\lvert B^{*}\rvert
. Here,
W_{1}
is an isometry on
\ker{A^{*}}^{\perp}
with
\operatorname{ran}{W}_{1}=\ker{A}^{\perp}
, and similarly,
W_{2}
is an isometry on
\ker{B^{*}}^{\perp}
with
\operatorname{ran}{W_{2}}=\operatorname{ran}{B}^{\perp}
. Since
\ker{A}=\{0\}
by assumption, it follows that
W_{1}
is surjective. Since
\operatorname{ran}{A}^{\perp}=\ker{A^{*}}=\{0\}
by assumption and
Lemma

A.6
, it follows that
W_{1}
is an isometry on all of
\mathcal{H}
. Hence
W_{1}
is a surjective isometry on
\mathcal{H}
, that is a Hilbert space isomorphism. Similarly,
W_{2}
is a Hilbert space isomorphism, and therefore so is
W_{2}W_{1}^{-1}
. Now,
AA^{*}=BB^{*}
implies
\lvert A^{*}\rvert=\lvert B^{*}\rvert
. Thus,
B^{*}=W_{2}\lvert B^{*}\rvert=W_{2}\lvert A^{*}\rvert=W_{2}W_{1}^{-1}W_{1}\lvert A^{*}\rvert=W_{2}W_{1}^{-1}A^{*}
. We conclude that
B=AQ
, where
Q\coloneqq(W_{1}W_{2}^{-1})^{*}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a Hilbert space isomorphism.
∎

A.2
Unbounded operators

For
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we denote by
A^{\dagger}
the Moore–Penrose inverse of
A
, also known as the generalised inverse and pseudo-inverse of
A
, c.f.
[
15
, Definition 2.2]
,
[
13
, Section B.2]
or
[
19
, Definition 3.5.7]
. It holds that
A^{\dagger}
is bounded if and only if
\operatorname{ran}{A}
is closed, c.f.
[
15
, Proposition 2.4]
. If
A
is injective, then
A^{\dagger}=A^{-1}
on
\operatorname{ran}{A}
.

Lemma A.14
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces. Suppose
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. If
\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{A_{2}}
, then there exists
C>0
such that
\lVert A_{2}^{\dagger}k\rVert\leq C\lVert A_{1}^{\dagger}k\rVert
for all
k\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
.

The proof is a modification of the arguments in the proof of
[
13
, Proposition B.1(ii)]
.

Proof.

Let us first assume that
A_{2}
is injective, so that
A_{2}^{\dagger}=A_{2}^{-1}
on
\operatorname{ran}{A_{2}}
.
We must show that there exists
C>0
such that
\lVert A_{2}^{-1}k\rVert\leq C\lVert A_{1}^{\dagger}k\rVert
for all
k\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
. We shall obtain a contradiction by supposing no
C>0
exists such that
\lVert A_{2}^{-1}k\rVert\leq C\lVert A_{1}^{\dagger}k\rVert
for all
k\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
. That is, we suppose that for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
k^{m}\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
such that
\lVert A_{2}^{-1}k^{m}\rVert>m\lVert A_{1}^{\dagger}k^{m}\rVert
. Since
k^{m}\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
and
\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{A_{2}}
, there exist
\tilde{h}_{1}^{m},\tilde{h}_{2}^{m}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\tilde{h}_{1}^{m}=A_{1}^{\dagger}k^{m}
and
\tilde{h}_{2}^{m}=A_{2}^{-1}k^{m}
. Thus,
A_{1}\tilde{h}^{m}_{1}=A_{2}\tilde{h}^{m}_{2}=k^{m}
. Define
h_{i}^{m}\coloneqq\tilde{h}_{i}^{m}/\lVert\tilde{h}_{1}^{m}\rVert
,
i=1,2
. Then
\lVert h_{1}^{m}\rVert=1
for all
m
and
\lVert h_{2}^{m}\rVert\rightarrow\infty
as
m\rightarrow\infty
. On the one hand, for every
C>0
there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
A_{2}h_{2}^{m}\not\in\{A_{2}h:\ \lVert h\rVert\leq C\}
for all
m>M
, by injectivity of
A_{2}
. On the other hand,
A_{1}h^{m}_{1}=k^{m}/\lVert\tilde{h}_{1}^{m}\rVert=A_{2}h^{m}_{2}
, hence
A_{2}h^{m}_{2}\in\{A_{1}h:\ \lVert h\rVert\leq 1\}
for all
m
. By
Lemma

A.10
,
A_{2}h^{m}_{2}\in\{A_{2}h:\ \lVert h\rVert\leq C\}
for all
m
and for some
m
-independent constant
C>0
, which is a contradiction.

Now let
A_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
be arbitrary. The subspace
\ker{A}_{2}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{H}
is closed and therefore a Hilbert space with respect to its subspace topology. Let us denote the restriction of
A_{2}
to
\ker{A_{2}}^{\perp}
by
\tilde{A}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\ker{A_{2}}^{\perp},\mathcal{K})
. Then
\tilde{A}_{2}
is injective and satisfies
\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{A}_{2}}=\operatorname{ran}{A_{2}}
. By construction of the Moore–Penrose inverse,
A_{2}^{\dagger}k=\tilde{A}_{2}^{-1}k\in\mathcal{H}
for
k\in\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{A}}_{2}=\operatorname{ran}{A_{2}}
. By the hypothesis
\operatorname{ran}{A}_{1}\subset\operatorname{ran}{A}_{2}
, we have
A_{2}^{\dagger}k=\tilde{A}_{2}^{-1}k\in\mathcal{H}
for
k\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
. From the previous part of the proof we can then conclude the existence of
C>0
such that
\lVert A_{2}^{\dagger}k\rVert=\lVert\tilde{A}_{2}^{-1}k\rVert\leq C\lVert A_{1}^{\dagger}k\rVert
for all
k\in\operatorname{ran}{A_{1}}
.
∎

Definition A.15

(
[
10
, Definition X.1.3]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. A linear operator
A:\operatorname{dom}{A}\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is said to be closed if its graph
\{(h,Ah):\ h\in\operatorname{dom}{A}\}
is closed in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space,
A:\operatorname{dom}{A}\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be closed and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Then,

(i)

AB
is closed,

(ii)

A+B
is closed,

(iii)

if
A
is also injective, then
A^{-1}:\operatorname{ran}{A}\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\operatorname{dom}{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
is closed.

Proof.

If
(h_{n},ABh_{n})\rightarrow(h,k)\in\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}
, then
(Bh_{n},ABh_{n})\rightarrow(Bh,k)
by continuity of
B
. Since
A
is closed,
Bh\in\operatorname{dom}{A}
, that is,
h\in\operatorname{dom}{AB}
, and
k=ABh
. This shows
item

(i)
.
Next, if
(h_{n},Ah_{n}+Bh_{n})\rightarrow(h,k)\in\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}
, then
Bh_{n}\rightarrow z
for some
z\in\mathcal{H}
by continuity of
B
, and
(h_{n},Ah_{n})\rightarrow(h,k-z)
. Since
A
is closed,
h\in\operatorname{dom}{A}=\operatorname{dom}{(A+B)}
and
Ah=k-z=k-Bh
. This shows
item

(ii)
.
Finally, if
A
is also injective, then we have
\{(h,Ah):\ h\in\operatorname{dom}{A}\}=\{(A^{-1}k,k):\ k\in\operatorname{ran}{A}\}
, and this set is closed if and only if the set
\{(k,A^{-1}k):\ k\in\operatorname{ran}{A}\}=\{(k,A^{-1}k):\ k\in\operatorname{dom}{A^{-1}}\}
is closed. This shows
item

(iii)
.
∎

Lemma A.17

(Closed graph theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Banach space. If
A:\operatorname{dom}{A}\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
satisfies
\operatorname{dom}{A}=\mathcal{H}
, then
A
is continuous if and only if
A
is closed.

Proof.

It follows by definition of continuity that
A
is closed for
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. For the converse, see
[
10
, Theorem III.12.6]
.
∎

Definition A.18

(
[
10
, Definition X.1.5]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be separable Hilbert spaces and
A:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
be a densely defined linear operator on
\mathcal{H}
. Then we define

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}{A^{*}}\coloneqq\{k\in\mathcal{K}:\ h\mapsto\langle Ah,k\rangle\text{ is a bounded linear functional on }\operatorname{dom}{A}\}.

As
\operatorname{dom}{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
is dense, if
k\in\mathcal{K}
, there exists by the Riesz representation theorem some
f\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\langle Ah,k\rangle=\langle h,f\rangle
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. We define
A^{*}:\operatorname{dom}{A^{*}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
by setting
A^{*}k=f
.

Lemma A.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. If
A,B
, and
AB:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
are densely defined, then

(i)

(AB)^{*}\supset B^{*}A^{*}
,

(ii)

If
B^{*}A^{*}
is bounded, then
(AB)^{*}=B^{*}A^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\operatorname{dom}{B^{*}A^{*}}
and
h\in\operatorname{dom}{AB}
. Since
k\in\operatorname{dom}{A^{*}}
and
Bh\in\operatorname{dom}{A}
,
\langle ABh,k\rangle=\langle Bh,A^{*}k\rangle.
Since
A^{*}k\in\operatorname{dom}{B^{*}}
and
h\in\operatorname{dom}{B}
,
\langle Bh,A^{*}k\rangle=\langle h,B^{*}A^{*}k\rangle.
Thus
\langle ABh,k\rangle=\langle h,B^{*}A^{*}k\rangle
. Hence
h\mapsto\langle ABh,k\rangle
is bounded and
(AB)^{*}k=B^{*}A^{*}k
, proving part
(i)
. If
B^{*}A^{*}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, then
\operatorname{dom}{(AB)^{*}}\subset\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{dom}{B^{*}A^{*}}
, showing part
(ii)
.
∎

Definition A.20

(
[
10
, Definitions X.2.1 and X.2.3]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. A densely defined operator
A:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is said to be symmetric if
\langle Ah,k\rangle=\langle h,Ak\rangle
for all
h,k\in\operatorname{dom}{A}
.
If
A=A^{*}
, then
A
is said to be self-adjoint.

Remark A.21
.

Note that
A=A^{*}
if and only if
A
is symmetric and additionally
\operatorname{dom}{A}=\operatorname{dom}{A^{*}}
holds.

Lemma A.22

(
[
10
, Proposition X.2.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
A
be a symmetric operator on
\mathcal{H}
.

(i)

If
\operatorname{ran}{A}
is dense, then
A
is injective.

(ii)

If
A=A^{*}
and
A
is injective, then
\operatorname{ran}{A}
is dense and
A^{-1}
is well-defined on
\operatorname{ran}{A}
and self-adjoint.

(iii)

If
\operatorname{dom}{A}=\mathcal{H}
, then
A=A^{*}
and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

(iv)

If
\operatorname{ran}{A}=\mathcal{H}
, then
A=A^{*}
and
A^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

Lemma A.23
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
be nonnegative.
If
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}\subset\mathcal{H}
densely, then the following hold.

(i)

\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}>0
.

(ii)

\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}:\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}:\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
are bijective and self-adjoint operators that are unbounded if
\dim{\mathcal{H}}
is unbounded.

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.22
(i)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
and hence
\mathcal{C}_{1}
are injective, so
(i)
holds. Furthermore, by
Lemma

A.22
(ii)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}
are bijective and self-adjoint. The inverse of a compact operator on its range in an infinite-dimensional space is unbounded, hence
(ii)
holds.
∎

Condition
(i)
of the Feldman–Hajek theorem,
Theorem

3.1
, can be stated equivalently as follows.

Lemma A.24
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
injective. Then
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
if and only if
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
is a well-defined invertible operator in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
. Then
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}
is well-defined and bijective. By
Lemma

A.16
(iii)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
closed, being the inverse of a bounded, hence closed, operator. By
Lemma

A.16
(i)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}
is closed, and by
Lemma

A.17
, it is bounded.
Conversely, if
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is invertible, then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}h:h\in\mathcal{H}\}=\{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}h:h\in\mathcal{H}\}=\{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}h:h\in\mathcal{H}\}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}.

∎

Appendix B
Proofs of results

B.1
Proofs of Section
3

See
3.4

Proof of
Lemma

3.4
.

By the Feldman–Hajek theorem,
Theorem

3.1
,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
. Thus, by
Lemma

A.24
,
A\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}
is a well-defined bounded and invertible operator, and by
Theorem

3.1
(iii)
,
AA^{*}-I
is Hilbert–Schmidt. That is, there exists a sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i}\subset\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and ONB
(w_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
such that,

\displaystyle AA^{*}-I=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}w_{i}\otimes w_{i},

i.e.,

\displaystyle AA^{*}w_{i}=(1+\lambda_{i})w_{i}.

(24)

As
A
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, so are
A^{*}
and
AA^{*}
. Furthermore,
AA^{*}\geq 0
, hence
AA^{*}>0
by
Lemma

A.4
, which shows that
\lambda_{i}>-1
for all
i
, which proves
item

(i)
holds.

By applying
A^{-1}
,
A^{-1}(A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}
and
(A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}
to (
24
) and rearranging, we obtain respectively,

\displaystyle A^{*}AA^{-1}w_{i}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda_{i})A^{-1}w_{i},

(25)

\displaystyle A^{-1}(A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}w_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{i}}A^{-1}w_{i},

(26)

\displaystyle(A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}w_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{i}}w_{i}.

(27)

By (
27
),
v_{i}\coloneqq(1+\lambda_{i})^{1/2}A^{-1}w_{i}
satisfies,

\displaystyle\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=(1+\lambda_{i})^{1/2}(1+\lambda_{j})^{1/2}\langle(A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}w_{i},w_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},

and, for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle A^{-1}h,v_{i}\rangle v_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(1+\lambda_{i})\langle h,(A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}w_{i}\rangle A^{-1}w_{i}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}\sum_{i}\langle h,w_{i}\rangle w_{i}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}h,

where we used that
A^{-1}
is continuous and
(w_{i})_{i}
is an ONB. Hence,
(v_{i})_{i}
is an ONB. Now, (
25
), (
26
) and (
27
) become

\displaystyle(A^{*}A-I)v_{i}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}v_{i},

\displaystyle(A^{-1}(A^{-1})^{*}-I)v_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}v_{i},

\displaystyle((A^{-1})^{*}A^{-1}-I)w_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}w_{i}.

Notice that
\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
, since
1+\lambda_{i}\rightarrow 1
and
(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
. This proves
items

(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
.

Finally, we prove the statements about the domains of the leftmost operators in
items

(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
.
By
Lemma

A.23
(ii)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
is self-adjoint. By
Lemma

A.19
(i)
,
A^{*}\supset\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
and the latter operator is defined on
\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
by the definition of composition of linear operators, c.f.
Section

1.5
. This shows that the leftmost operator in
item

(i)
, and by symmetry also in
item

(iii)
, is defined on the dense subspace
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}
. Since
A
is boundedly invertible,
\overline{A^{-1}(\mathcal{D})}=A^{-1}(\overline{\mathcal{D}})=\mathcal{H}
for any dense set
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{H}
. This shows that
A^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. Since
\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}}=A^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}})
, this proves that the leftmost operator in
item

(ii)
, and by symmetry also in
item

(iv)
, is densely defined.
∎

See
3.7

Proof of
Proposition

3.7
.

By
Lemma

3.4
with
\mathcal{C}_{1}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
, there exists an eigenvalue sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
and ONBs
(w_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(v_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
such that
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}
and
Lemma

3.4
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
,
(iv)
hold. In particular, by
Lemma

3.4
(i)
and the definition of
R(\cdot\|\cdot)
in (
10
),
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}})=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}w_{i}\otimes w_{i}
.
By (
4c
), it holds on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}-I=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+H)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}-I=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}+\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}-I.

Now
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}-I\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, hence it is defined on all of
\mathcal{H}
. The operator
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
is extended by the identity operator
I
. Thus,

\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}-I\subset\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}.

By the uniqueness of extensions of continuous functions on the dense set
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
, this implies together with
Lemma

3.4
(iv)
that (
11a
) holds.
The proof of (
11b
) is similar: by (
4c
), it holds on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(-H)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2},

and combining this with
Lemma

3.4
(ii)
, (
11b
) follows by uniqueness of the extension.

We now prove the stated properties of the eigenvalues and eigenvectors.
Recall that by (
3
),
H\in\mathcal{B}_{00,n}(\mathcal{H})
is self-adjoint and non-negative. Hence the operator
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}=(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H^{1/2})^{*}
is also self-adjoint and non-negative, which implies that
(\tfrac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\ell^{2}((-1,0])
, and thus that
(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}((-1,0])
. We thus may order
(\lambda_{i})_{i}
in a nondecreasing manner. Since
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}
is injective on
\mathcal{H}
, it follows by applying
Lemma

A.8
twice with
A\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H^{1/2}
and
A\leftarrow H^{1/2}
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H^{1/2})^{*}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rank}\left({H^{1/2}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right).

Therefore,
(\tfrac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
contains exactly
\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)\leq n
many nonzero entries.
It follows directly from (
11a
), (
11b
) and the fact that
\lambda_{i}\not=0
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
, that
w_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
and
v_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
. By
Lemma

A.1
, we can extend
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)}
to an ONB
(w_{i}^{\prime})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
with
(w_{i}^{\prime})_{i}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
and
w_{i}^{\prime}=w_{i}
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
. We now replace
w_{i}
by
w_{i}^{\prime}
and
v_{i}
by
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}^{\prime}
for
i>\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
. After this replacement, the equations (
11a
) and (
11b
) and
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}
for all
i
remain valid, and we now have
w_{i},v_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
for all
i
.

By
Lemma

3.4
(i)
and the fact that
(w_{i})_{i}
lies in the Cameron–Martin space, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}=(1+\lambda_{i})w_{i},\quad i\in\mathbb{N}.

Applying
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
to both sides of the equation yields (
11c
).
∎

See
3.8

Proof of
Theorem

3.8
.

We use the expressions for the KL and Rényi divergence of
[
25
, Theorem 14, Theorem 15]
. While they are stated for infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces only, it is noted in
[
26
]
that these expressions also hold for finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces; see the remarks after
[
26
, Theorem 3]
.
By
Lemma

A.19
(i)
,
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})^{*}=\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
.
The statements in the theorem now follow immediately from the expressions in
[
25
, Theorem 14, Theorem 15]
, because for
S\coloneqq-R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
R(\cdot\|\cdot)
is defined in (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}(I-S)\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{1/2})^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{2},

and
I-(1-\rho)S=\rho I+(1-\rho)(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))
for
0\leq\rho\leq 1
.
∎

B.2
Proofs of Section
4

See
4.1

Proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

Given that
xf^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x\neq 0
, it follows that
f^{\prime}(x)<0
for
x<0
and
f^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x>0
. This implies, by continuity of
f
, that
f^{\prime}(0)=0
. Hence
f
has a global minimum only at
x=0
and
f\geq 0
. Thus, also
\mathcal{L}_{f}\geq 0
.
By the Lipschitz assumption on
f^{\prime}
at 0, there exists
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
M_{0}>0
such that
f^{\prime}(x)=f^{\prime}(x)-f^{\prime}(0)\leq M_{0}\lvert x\rvert
for
\lvert x\rvert\leq\varepsilon
. For
\lvert y\rvert\leq\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle f(y)=f(y)-f(0)=\int_{0}^{y}f^{\prime}(x)\operatorname{d}\!{x}\leq\int_{0}^{y}M_{0}\lvert x\rvert\operatorname{d}\!{x}=\frac{1}{2}M_{0}y^{2}.

Let
(x_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
. For
N
large enough, its tail
(x_{i})_{i>N}
lies in
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, so that the inequality above implies
\sum_{i>N}f(x_{i})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}M\sum_{i>N}x_{i}^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}M\lVert x\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{2}
. For
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
we have
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
and its eigenvalue sequence is square-summable. Hence
\mathcal{L}_{f}<\infty
by the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{f}
in (
17a
). This proves
item

(i)
. For
item

(ii)
, we note
f(\eta(0))=f(0)=0
. Furthermore, we compute
\eta^{\prime}(x)=-(1+x)^{-2}
and, by the fact that
f\in\mathscr{F}
,
x(f\circ\eta)^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{1+x}\frac{-x}{1+x}f^{\prime}(\frac{-x}{1+x})>0
for
x\not=0
. By the assumption on
f
,
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}f(\eta(x))=\lim_{x\rightarrow-1}f(x)=\infty
. Finally,
\eta
is smooth, so
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are Lipschitz at 0. Therefore,
f^{\prime}\circ\eta
is Lipschitz at 0 as the composition of Lipschitz functions at 0, and
(f\circ\eta)^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}\circ\eta)\eta^{\prime}
is Lipschitz at 0 as the product of two Lipschitz functions at 0.
∎

See
4.3

Proof of
Lemma

4.3
.

Notice that
f_{\textup{KL}}
,
f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, which implies
f_{\textup{KL}}^{\prime}
and
f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}^{\prime}
are locally Lipschitz on
(-1,\infty)
. We compute
xf_{\textup{KL}}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2(1+x)}>0
for
x\not=0
and

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\rho(1-\rho)}\left[\frac{\rho-1}{1+x}+\frac{1-\rho}{\rho+(1-\rho)(1+x)}\right]=\frac{x}{2(1+x)[\rho+(1-\rho)(1+x)]}.

Hence
xf^{\prime}_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(x)>0
for
x\not=0
.
Furthermore,
f_{\textup{KL}}(0)=0=f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(0)
and
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}f_{\textup{KL}}(x)=\infty=\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(x)
, so
f_{\textup{KL}},f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}\in\mathscr{F}
.

The first equations in
items

(i)
and
(ii)
follow from
Theorem

3.8
.
With
(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the eigenvalues of
R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, it holds by (
13
) that

\displaystyle\det\nolimits_{2}(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(1+\lambda_{i})\exp(-\lambda_{i})=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\exp(-2f_{\textup{KL}}(\lambda_{i}))=\exp\biggr(-2\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{\textup{KL}}(\lambda_{i})\biggr),

which proves that
D_{\textup{KL}}(\nu||\mu)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{\textup{KL}}(\lambda_{i})=\mathcal{L}_{f_{\textup{KL}}}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})
. Hence
item

(i)
holds.

By the spectral mapping theorem—see e.g.
[
29
, Theorem VII.1(e)]
for a version that does not assume that
\mathcal{H}
is defined over the complex field
\mathbb{C}
—the eigenvalues of
I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})
are
(1+\lambda_{i})_{i}
, and the eigenvalues of
A_{\rho}\coloneqq\bigl(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1})\bigr)^{\rho-1}\bigl(\rho I+(1-\rho)(I+R(\mathcal{C}_{2}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}))\bigr)
are
(\gamma_{i})_{i}
, with
\gamma_{i}\coloneqq(1+\lambda_{i})^{\rho-1}(\rho+(1-\rho)(1+\lambda_{i}))
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The eigenvalues of
A_{\rho}-I
are then
(\gamma_{i}-1)_{i}
and by (
12
),

\displaystyle\det(A_{\rho})

\displaystyle=\det(I+(A_{\rho}-I))=\prod_{i}(1+(\gamma_{i}-1))

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{i}\log(\gamma_{i})\right)=\exp\left(2\rho(1-\rho)\sum_{i}f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(\lambda_{i})\right).

This shows
item

(ii)
holds.
Since
\lim_{x\rightarrow-1}f_{\textup{KL}}(x)=\infty=\lim_{x\rightarrow-1}f_{\textup{Ren},\rho}(x)
,
item

(iii)
now follows directly from
items

(i)
and
(ii)
and
Lemma

4.1
(ii)
.
∎

See
4.6

Proof of
Lemma

4.6
.

(i)
If
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}}
, then
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K
is well-defined in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and thus so is
\mathfrak{X}
. By
Lemma

A.19
(i)
,
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}=K^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
, whence
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=K^{*}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}
.

(ii)
If
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}\geq 0
, then
\langle KK^{*}h,h\rangle\leq\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}h,h\rangle
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Hence
\lVert K^{*}h\rVert\leq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}h\rVert
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. By
Lemma

A.10
,
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
. By
item

(i)
,
\mathfrak{X}
is well-defined in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
. Furthermore,
\langle\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}h,\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}h\rangle=\langle\mathcal{C}h,h\rangle\geq 0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Since
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}\subset\mathcal{H}
densely, it follows that
\langle\mathfrak{X}h,h\rangle\geq 0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Conversely, if
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and
\mathfrak{X}\geq 0
, then using
item

(i)
we find
\langle\mathcal{C}h,h\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}h,\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}h\rangle\geq 0
for
h\in\mathcal{H}
.

(iii)
The implication
(iii)(a)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(c)
holds by definition of
\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
in (
8
) and
Theorem

3.1
(i)
.

Now,
(iii)(c)
implies
(iii)(b)
. Indeed,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}^{\perp}=\ker{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}
and
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}^{\perp}=\ker{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\{0\}
by
Lemma

A.6
and injectivity of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
. Furthermore,
\ker{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}=\ker{\mathcal{C}}
by
Lemma

A.7
. Thus, if
(iii)(c)
holds, then
\mathcal{C}
is nonnegative and injective, hence positive, by
Lemma

A.4
.

Next, we show that
(iii)(b)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(a)
. By (
8
) and
Theorem

3.1
, we only need to show that
\mathcal{C}
is trace-class and that
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})^{*}-I
is Hilbert–Schmidt. Since
\mathcal{C}_{1}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
KK^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{00,r}(\mathcal{H})\subset L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, also
\mathcal{C}\in L_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
By
Lemma

A.19
(ii)

(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})^{*}=\mathcal{C}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})^{*}-I=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}-I=\mathfrak{X}-I=-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}.

The outermost operators
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})^{*}-I
and
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
are bounded and defined on all of
\mathcal{H}
. Since
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}\subset\mathcal{H}
densely, it follows that
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})^{*}-I=-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
on
\mathcal{H}
.
Since
K
has finite rank, so does
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
.
We conclude that
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2})^{*}-I\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, so that
(iii)(b)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(a)
.

The equivalence of
(iii)(c)
and
(iii)(d)
follows from
item

(ii)
.

Finally, we show
(iii)(d)
and
(iii)(e)
are equivalent. Note that
(iii)(d)
implies
\mathcal{C}>0
by the already proven equivalence
(iii)(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)(d)
. Also
(iii)(e)
implies
\mathcal{C}>0
. Indeed,
\mathcal{C}\geq 0
by
item

(ii)
, and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
is injective as a composition of injective maps, by
item

(i)
. Thus, by
Lemma

A.4
,
\mathcal{C}>0
if
(iii)(e)
holds.
We therefore assume that
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
,
\mathfrak{X}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{C}>0
, and show that
\mathfrak{X}
is invertible if and only if
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}
.
Since
\mathfrak{X}\geq 0
,
\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}
exists. By
(i)
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{1/2})^{*}
.
Thus, both
\mathcal{C}^{1/2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}
are (possibly non-self adjoint) square roots of
\mathcal{C}
. Now,
\mathcal{C}
is self-adjoint and positive, hence
\overline{\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}}=\ker{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}
by
Lemma

A.6
. By
Lemma

A.13
applied with
A\leftarrow\mathcal{C}^{1/2}
and
B\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}
, there exists a Hilbert space isomorphism
Q\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}^{1/2}Q
. From this we conclude two facts.
On the one hand, if
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
, so that
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2}
is boundedly invertible by
Lemma

A.24
, then this implies that
\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}^{1/2}Q
is boundedly invertible. Hence
\mathfrak{X}
is boundedly invertible as the composition of boundedly invertible operators.
On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{X}
and hence
\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}
is boundedly invertible, then
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}Q}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
. We conclude that
(iii)(d)
and
(iii)(e)
are equivalent.

(iv)
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}\geq 0
. By
items

(ii)
and
(i)
,
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and
\mathfrak{X}\coloneqq I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is a well-defined and nonnegative operator. By injectivity of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
, we have that
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K
and
K
have the same rank. Thus, by
Lemma

A.8
,
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
and
K
have the same rank. We then diagonalise the nonnegative and self-adjoint operator
I-\mathfrak{X}=(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
as
\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}d_{i}^{2}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
, where
d_{i}^{2}\geq d_{i+1}^{2}>0
and
(e_{i})_{i}
is an orthonormal sequence in
\mathcal{H}
. By nonnegativity of
\mathfrak{X}
, we have
1-d_{i}^{2}=\langle\mathfrak{X}e_{i},e_{i}\rangle\geq 0
, that is,
d_{i}^{2}\in(0,1]
, for each
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
.

Furthermore,
\mathfrak{X}
is invertible if and only if
d_{i}^{2}\not=1
for each
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
by
Lemma

A.9
(i)
applied with
\delta_{i}\leftarrow-d_{i}^{2}
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
and
\delta_{i}\leftarrow 0
otherwise. Using
(iii)(e)
of
item

(iii)
, it follows that the equivalent statements of
item

(iii)
hold if and only if
(d_{i}^{2})_{i}\subset(0,1)
.

Suppose the additional assumptions
\mathcal{C}>0
and
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
hold. The latter assumption implies
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
, which in turn implies
\operatorname{ran}{(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
. Thus,
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
. Hence, the assumption
\mathcal{C}>0
and the fact that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
from
item

(i)
show
1-d_{i}^{2}=\langle\mathfrak{X}e_{i},e_{i}\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}e_{i},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}e_{i}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}e_{i},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}e_{i}\rangle>0
, showing
d_{i}^{2}<1
for each
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
.
∎

See
4.7

Proof of
Proposition

4.7
.

(i)
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
for some
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
. By the implication
(iii)(a)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(b)
and
(iii)(a)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(e)
of
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
, we have that
\mathcal{C}>0
, hence
\mathcal{C}
is injective, and that
\mathfrak{X}\coloneqq I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is a well-defined nonnegative, self-adjoint, and invertible operator.
We diagonalise
\mathfrak{X}
as
I-\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}d_{i}^{2}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
by
Lemma

4.6
(iv)
, where
(e_{i})_{i}\subset\mathcal{H}
is orthonormal and
(d_{i}^{2})_{i}\subset(0,1)
is nonincreasing.
By
Lemma

4.6
(i)
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
, which is the composition of three injective maps.
Using
Lemma

A.9
with
\delta_{i}\leftarrow-d_{i}^{2}
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
and
\delta_{i}\leftarrow 0
otherwise, the inverse of
\mathcal{C}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{X}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\left(I+\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\frac{d_{i}^{2}}{1-d_{i}^{2}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2},

where
Z\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\sqrt{\frac{d_{i}^{2}}{1-d_{i}^{2}}}e_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i}
for any choice of ONB
(\varphi_{i})_{i}
of
\mathbb{R}^{r}
. Since
(d_{i}^{2})_{i}\in(0,1)
, we have
\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is injective and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}
for some
Z\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. Since
I+ZZ^{*}\geq I
,
I+ZZ^{*}
is invertible. Thus,
\mathcal{C}^{-1}
is the composition of three injective operators, and we can invert
\mathcal{C}^{-1}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{-1}}=\mathcal{H}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}^{-1})^{-1}=\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}\right)^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}.

By
Lemma

A.8
,
\operatorname{rank}\left({ZZ^{*}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)
, and we diagonalise
ZZ^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)}b_{i}^{2}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for
b_{i}^{2}\geq b_{i+1}^{2}>0
and
(g_{i})_{i}
an orthonormal sequence in
\mathcal{H}
. Then, by
Lemma

A.9
applied with
\delta_{i}\leftarrow b_{i}^{2}
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)
and
\delta_{i}\leftarrow 0
otherwise, it follows that
(I+ZZ^{*})^{-1}=I-\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\left(I-\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}\right)\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}\right)\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}.

We see that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
with
K\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\frac{b_{i}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i}
for any choice of ONB
(\varphi_{i})_{i}
of
\mathbb{R}^{r}
. Hence,
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
and
\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({Z}\right)
. It remains to show that
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
. By the implication
(iii)(e)
\Rightarrow
(iii)(a)
of
Lemma

4.6
(iii)
, it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{X}\coloneqq I-(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}
is nonnegative and invertible. We have
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}KK^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
. Now,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}\subset\mathcal{H}
densely and
\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Thus,
Lemma

A.19
(i)
implies
(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}\supset K^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
and hence
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}K)^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}
. It follows that

\mathfrak{X}=I-\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}\frac{b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}g_{i}\otimes g_{i}.

Lemma

A.9
(i)
-
(ii)
, applied with
\delta_{i}\leftarrow\frac{-b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}
for
i\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)
and
\delta_{i}\leftarrow 0
otherwise, implies that
\mathfrak{X}
is nonnegative and invertible, since
\frac{-b_{i}^{2}}{1+b_{i}^{2}}>-1
for all

.

(ii)
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}-KK^{*}
for some
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
with
\operatorname{ran}{K}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{E}(m_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
.
We first show that
\mathcal{C}
is injective and
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
.
By
item

(i)
,
\mathcal{C}
is injective and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}

(iii)(e)

\Rightarrow
(iii)(b)
that
\mathcal{C}>0
. Using

4.6
(iv)
, we diagonalise
\mathfrak{X}=I-\sum_{i=1}^{\operatorname{rank}\left({K}\right)}d_{i}^{2}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
where

\mathcal{H}
. It follows that

maps
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}
onto itself. Hence
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}\mathfrak{X}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}

from
Lemma

4.6
(i)
.

Next, we show that we may write
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}+UU^{*}
on

for some

satisfying

.
Let

. This shows we may write
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}(I+ZZ^{*})\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}+\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}ZZ^{*}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
. With

, and

by injectivity of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1/2}
. By

A.19
(i)
, we have

on

on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}

.

For the converse implication, assume that
\mathcal{C}

\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{-1}+UU^{*}
on
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
for some
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
.
With

it holds that

by injectivity of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1/2}
, and

on

. By

As in the proof of the ‘only if’ statement in

, we can diagonalise

and write

of

.

(iii)
The ‘if’ direction holds by

(ii)
and the implication

\Rightarrow

of

4.6
(iii)
.
The ‘only if’ direction follows from

∎

See
