Proof of
Lemma

4.12
.

By
Corollary

4.10
(i)
and
Corollary

4.11
,
(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1}\in\mathcal{E}
for
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
.
By
Lemma

3.4
(ii)
applied with
\mathcal{C}_{1}\leftarrow(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I
is densely defined and there exists an ONB
(e_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
and eigenvalue sequence
(\gamma_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I\subset((\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})^{*}((\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})-I=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i},

and by comparing with the expansion in
Lemma

3.4
(i)
,
\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
has the same eigenvalues as
R(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})^{-1})
, counting multiplicities.
Using (
11b
), the leftmost operator can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I+\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\subset(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I+\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=g(U).

Since
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+UU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-I
is densely defined, the above continuous extension is unique, which shows that
g(U)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
.
By
Proposition

3.7
,
\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}\left({g(U)}\right)\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}\right)+\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}\right)\leq\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)+r.

Furthermore,
\operatorname{ran}{g(U)}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
.
For
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\gamma_{i}\not=0
, this implies
e_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
.
By
Lemma

A.1
, we can extend
(e_{i})_{i:\gamma_{i}\not=0}
to a sequence in
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
which is an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
. Replacing
(e_{i})_{i}
with this sequence, we still have
g(U)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
and now
e_{i}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
for all
i
.
∎

See
4.13

Proof of
Lemma

4.13
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
.
We first show that the linear map
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
given by
V\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
is bounded, and then identify this map as the Fréchet derivative of
g
at
U
.
Let
V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. By
Lemma

A.6
,
\dim{\ker{V^{*}}^{\perp}}=\operatorname{rank}\left({V}\right)\leq r
. Then there exists an ONB
(e_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
for which
\operatorname{span}{\left(e_{i},\ i\leq r\right)}
contains
\ker{V^{*}}^{\perp}
.
We have,

\displaystyle\lVert UV^{*}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\sum_{i}\lVert UV^{*}e_{i}\rVert^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert UV^{*}e_{i}\rVert^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert U\rVert^{2}\lVert V^{*}\rVert^{2}=r\lVert U\rVert^{2}\lVert V\rVert^{2},

where we use the definition of the
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
-norm, the inclusion
\ker{V^{*}}^{\perp}\subset\operatorname{span}{\left(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\right)}
, the definition of the operator norm, and
\lVert V^{*}\rVert=\lVert V\rVert
by
[
10
, Proposition VI.1.4(b)]
.
This also shows
\lVert VU^{*}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\sqrt{r}\lVert V\rVert\lVert U\rVert
and
\lVert VV^{*}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\sqrt{r}\lVert V\rVert^{2}
.
Thus, using the triangle inequality and the fact
\lVert TS\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\lVert ST\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\lVert T\rVert\lVert S\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}
for any
T,S\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert^{2}\lVert UV^{*}+VU^{*}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq 2\sqrt{r}\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert^{2}\lVert U\rVert\lVert V\rVert.

It follows that
V\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
is bounded.
We have by (
20
),

\displaystyle g(U+V)-g(U)=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}((U+V)(U+V)^{*}-UU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(VV^{*}+UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}.

Using once more the fact
\lVert TS\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\lVert ST\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\lVert T\rVert\lVert S\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}
for any
T,S\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, and the bound
\lVert VV^{*}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\sqrt{r}\lVert V\rVert^{2}
proven above, it follows that

\displaystyle\lVert g(U+V)-g(U)-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(VV^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert^{2}\lVert VV^{*}\rVert_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\sqrt{r}\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\rVert^{2}\lVert V\rVert^{2}.

Dividing by
\lVert V\rVert
and letting
\lVert V\rVert\rightarrow 0
, this shows that
g
is differentiable and has the stated derivative.

To show differentiability of
F_{f}
, let
x=(x_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,\infty))
,
y=(y_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and define
c_{x}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by
(c_{x})_{i}=f^{\prime}(x_{i})
. By the assumption
f\in\mathscr{F}
,
f^{\prime}
is Lipschitz continuous in some neighbourhood
(-a,a)
of
0
, with
a>0
and with Lipschitz constant
M_{0}
. Let us take
N_{x}
so large that
\lvert x_{i}\rvert<a/2
for
i>N_{x}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. By differentiability of
f
, we can choose
\delta_{x,\varepsilon}>0
such that
x_{i}+z\in(-1,\infty)
and
\lvert(f(x_{i}+z)-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})z)\rvert<\varepsilon\lvert z\rvert
for
\lvert z\rvert<\delta_{x,\varepsilon}
and
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
. We then have for
\lVert y\rVert<\min(\delta_{x,\varepsilon},a/2,\varepsilon)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\lvert F_{f}(x+y)-F_{f}(x)-\langle c_{x},y\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\lvert\sum_{i}f(x_{i}+y_{i})-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\frac{1}{\lvert y_{i}\rvert}\lvert f(x_{i}+y_{i})-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i}\rvert

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\sum_{i>N_{x}}\lvert f(x_{i}+y_{i})-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i}\rvert.

As
\lvert y_{i}\rvert<\delta_{x,\varepsilon}
for
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
, the first term is bounded from above by
N_{x}\varepsilon
. For the second term, by the mean value theorem, for each
i>N_{x}
we can find
c_{i}\in[x_{i}-\lvert y_{i}\rvert,x_{i}+\lvert y_{i}\rvert]\subset(-a,a)
such that,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\sum_{i>N_{x}}\lvert f(x_{i}+y_{i})-f(x_{i})-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i}\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\sum_{i>N_{x}}\lvert f^{\prime}(c_{i})y_{i}-f^{\prime}(x_{i})y_{i}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\sum_{i>N_{x}}M_{0}\lvert c_{i}-x_{i}\rvert\lvert y_{i}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\sum_{i>N_{x}}M_{0}\lvert y_{i}\rvert^{2}\leq M_{0}\lVert y\rVert\leq M_{0}\varepsilon,

where we used the Lipschitz continuity of
f^{\prime}
in
(-a,a)
in the first inequality, and the fact that
c_{i}\in[x_{i}-\lvert y_{i}\rvert,x_{i}+\lvert y_{i}\rvert]
in the second inequality.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lVert y\rVert}\lvert F_{f}(x+y)-F_{f}(x)-\langle c_{x},y\rangle\rvert\leq(N_{x}+M_{0})\varepsilon,

from which we conclude that
F_{f}^{\prime}(x)
exists and
F_{f}^{\prime}(x)=c_{x}
.
∎

See
4.16

Proof of
Proposition

4.16
.

We need to relate the Fréchet differentiability of the composition
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
, being the composition of the eigenvalue map
\Lambda^{m}
on
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
as defined in
Section

1.5
and a symmetric function
\mathcal{G}
, to Fréchet differentiability of
\mathcal{G}
itself. To do so, we use
[
22
, Theorem 1.1]
, which states this for the case that
\Lambda^{m}
is defined on the space of symmetric matrices instead of
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})
. Therefore, we identify this space of symmetric matrices with
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
. The details of this identification are described in the following.

Any statement regarding differentiability in this proof should be understood as Fréchet differentiability.
Let us write Sym(
m
) for the symmetric matrices on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
endowed with the trace-inner product:
\langle A,B\rangle_{\text{Sym(}m\text{)}}=\operatorname{tr}\,\left({BA}\right)
for symmetric matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
.
Let
(\varphi_{i})_{i}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Define
\Phi:L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\text{Sym(}m\text{)}
by
\Phi(X)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\langle Xe_{j},e_{i}\rangle\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{j}
and let
X\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\Phi
is an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces and by linearity of
\Phi
we have
\Phi^{\prime}(X)(X_{2})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\langle X_{2}e_{j},e_{i}\rangle\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{j}
for all
X_{2}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Furthermore,
\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1}
is the eigenvalue map on Sym(
m
), where the eigenvalues are ordered in a nonincreasing way. We note that
\Phi(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1})_{i}(\Phi(X))\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i}
.
Because
\Omega
and
\mathcal{G}
are symmetric by hypothesis, we may apply
[
22
, Theorem 1.1]
, which states that
\mathcal{G}\circ(\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1})
is differentiable in
\Phi(X)
if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is differentiable in
\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(X))=\Lambda^{m}(X)
, in which case the derivative is given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1})^{\prime}(\Phi(X))=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\left(\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(X))\right)_{i}\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(X))_{i}\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i}.

By the chain rule,
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
is differentiable in
X
if and only if
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1}
is differentiable in
\Phi(X)
.
Thus, by the above display,
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
is differentiable in
X
if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is differentiable in
\Lambda^{m}(X)
. Another application of the chain rule, the expression for
\Phi^{\prime}
and the previous equation then finish the proof by showing that

\displaystyle\langle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(X),X_{2}\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})}

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1}\circ\Phi)^{\prime}(X),X_{2}\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}\circ\Phi^{-1})^{\prime}(\Phi(X)),\Phi^{\prime}(X)(X_{2})\Big\rangle_{\text{Sym(}m\text{)}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(X))_{i}\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i},\sum_{k,j=1}^{m}\langle X_{2}e_{j},e_{k}\rangle\varphi_{k}\otimes\varphi_{j}\Big\rangle_{\text{Sym(}m\text{)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(X))_{i}\langle X_{2}e_{i},e_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(X))e_{i}\otimes e_{i},X_{2}\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})},

for any
X_{2}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

See
4.18

Proof of
Proposition

4.18
.

Any statement regarding differentiability in this proof should be understood as Fréchet differentiability.
For
Y\in\mathcal{W}
, we have
\ker{Y}^{\perp}=\ker{Y^{*}}^{\perp}=\overline{\operatorname{ran}{Y}}=\operatorname{ran}{Y}
, by
Lemma

A.6
,
Y=Y^{*}
and the finite dimensionality of
\mathcal{Z}
.
Let
m\coloneqq\dim{\mathcal{Z}}
and extend
(e_{i})_{i}
to an ONB of
\mathcal{H}
. Note that the
m^{2}
operators
e_{i}\otimes e_{j}
,
i,j\leq m
, span the space
\mathcal{W}
. Therefore,
\dim{\mathcal{W}}=m^{2}<\infty
.
Because finite-dimensional spaces are closed, we can define
P_{\mathcal{Z}}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
, the orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{Z}
. Furthermore, we let
P_{m}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the orthogonal projector onto the first
m
coordinates of an
\ell^{2}
sequence. Thus,
P_{m}^{*}
is the natural embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
into
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
Proposition

4.16
is a statement on
L_{2}
spaces of finite dimension, we identify
\mathcal{W}
with
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
via
\Psi:L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\Psi(Y)\coloneqq P_{\mathcal{Z}}{\left.\kern-1.2ptY\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{Z}}}
.

We first prove the result for the case in which
\Lambda
orders the eigenvalues in nonincreasing absolute value. With
\Lambda^{m}
denoting the eigenvalue map on
L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
as defined in
Section

1.5
, we then have
\Lambda(Y)_{i}=\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(Y))_{i}
for all
i\leq m
and
Y\in\mathcal{W}
. This implies that

\displaystyle P_{m}\Lambda(Y)

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(Y)),\quad Y\in\mathcal{W},

\displaystyle\Lambda(Y)

\displaystyle=P_{m}^{*}\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(Y)),\quad Y\in\mathcal{W},

(28)

since any
Y\in\mathcal{W}
has at most
m
nonzero eigenvalues.
Let
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq FP_{m}^{*}
. If
\pi
is a permutation on
\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then
P_{m}^{*}x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},0,\ldots)
and
P_{m}^{*}(x_{\pi(i)})_{i}=(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(m)},0,\ldots)
, and by symmetry of
F
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}((x_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{m})=F(P_{m}^{*}(x_{\pi(i)})_{i})=F(P_{m}^{*}x)=\mathcal{G}(x)

showing that
\mathcal{G}
is a symmetric function on the symmetric domain
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
Furthermore, by definition of
\mathcal{G}
and (
28
)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}(Y)=(F\circ\Lambda)(Y)=(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})(\Psi(Y)),\quad Y\in\mathcal{W}.

(29)

By hypothesis,
F
is differentiable at
\Lambda(X)
, with
X\in\mathcal{W}
. The idea of the proof is to first use
Proposition

4.16
to conclude differentiability of
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
at
\Psi(X)
and then to use (
29
) and the chain rule to obtain differentiability of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
at
X
in the
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
norm topology.

In order to apply
Proposition

4.16
, we need to show that
\mathcal{G}
is differentiable in
\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))
.
By the hypothesis on
F
,
F
is differentiable at
\Lambda(X)
for
X\in\mathcal{W}
.
Furthermore,
P_{m}^{*}
is linear, hence differentiable, and
(P_{m}^{*})^{\prime}(x)(y)=P_{m}^{*}y
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Then, by (
28
) and the chain rule, the composition
F\circ P_{m}^{*}
is differentiable at
\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))
, and it holds for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\displaystyle\langle(FP_{m}^{*})^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))),y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle F^{\prime}(P_{m}^{*}\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))),(P_{m}^{*})^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X)))y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle F^{\prime}(P_{m}^{*}\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))),P_{m}^{*}y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle P_{m}F^{\prime}(P_{m}^{*}\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))),y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle P_{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X)),y\rangle,

where we use the chain rule in the first step, the expression for the derivative
(P_{m}^{*})^{\prime}
in the second step, the definition of the adjoint in the third step, and (
28
) in the final step.
That is,
\mathcal{G}
is differentiable at
\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X)))=P_{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

(30)

We may now apply
Proposition

4.16
to conclude that
\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}
is differentiable at
\Psi(X)
. To obtain an expression for the derivative, notice that by the fact that
e_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{Z}}
for
i\leq m
and by the hypothesised diagonalisation of
X
, we have
\Psi(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
, where the rank-1 operators
e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
are now understood to act only on
\mathcal{Z}
. With
Proposition

4.16
we thus also obtain the expression for the derivative

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(\Psi(X))=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\Lambda^{m}(\Psi(X)))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(P_{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))\right)_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathbb{R}},

where for the second equation we use (
30
). By definition of
P_{m}
,
\left(P_{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))\right)_{i}=F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))_{i}
for
i\leq m
. Hence,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(\Psi(X))=\sum_{i=1}^{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}.

(31)

Because
\Psi
is linear, hence differentiable, the chain rule and (
29
) show that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
is differentiable at
X
. To obtain the expression of the derivative, we use (
29
), the chain rule, the fact
\Psi^{\prime}(X)(Y)=\Psi(Y)
for
Y\in\mathcal{W}
and (
31
) to find

\displaystyle\langle{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\prime}(X),Y\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m}\circ\Psi)^{\prime}(X),Y\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(\Psi(X)),\Psi^{\prime}(X)(Y)\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})}

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathcal{G}\circ\Lambda^{m})^{\prime}(\Psi(X)),\Psi(Y)\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i},\Psi(Y)\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})}.

Since, for
Y\in\mathcal{W}
, it holds that
\operatorname{ran}{Y}\subset\mathcal{Z}
and
\ker{Y}^{\perp}=\operatorname{ran}{Y}
, we have
\ker{Y}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{Z}
. Thus, we have
\operatorname{ran}{\Psi(Y)}=Y(\mathcal{Z})=Y(\ker{Y}^{\perp})=\operatorname{ran}{Y}\subset\mathcal{Z}
as subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
. For
i\leq m
it holds that
e_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}
, hence
\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{i},\Psi(Y)\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{Z})}=\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{i},Y\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}
, where on the right hand side we interpret
e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
as acting on all of
\mathcal{H}
. For
i>m
, we have
e_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}
, so that
\operatorname{ran}{Y}\subset\mathcal{Z}
implies that
\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{i},Y\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
. Thus,

\displaystyle\langle{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\prime}(X),Y\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{m}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i},Y\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}F^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i},Y\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{\mathcal{H}})}.

This concludes the proof for the case that
\Lambda
orders the eigenvalues in a nonincreasing way.

Finally, let us denote by
\tilde{\Lambda}
an eigenvalue map on
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
which can assign any fixed but arbitrary ordering on the eigenvalues. Let
X\in\mathcal{W}
,
X=\sum_{i=1}\tilde{\Lambda}(X)_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
be given and assume that
F
is differentiable at
\tilde{\Lambda}(X)
.
Given
X
, there exists a permutation
\pi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that, for the eigenvalue map
\Lambda
from the previous part of the proof,
\tilde{\Lambda}(X)_{i}=\Lambda(X)_{\pi(i)}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
P_{\pi}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
denote the permutation operator
(P_{\pi}x)_{i}=x_{\pi(i)}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, so
P_{\pi}^{*}=P_{\pi}^{-1}
. Then
\tilde{\Lambda}(X)=(P_{\pi}\Lambda)(X)
and
X=\sum_{i}\Lambda_{\pi(i)}(X)e_{i}\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{i}\Lambda(X)_{i}e_{\pi^{-1}(i)}\otimes e_{\pi^{-1}(i)}.

By the previous part of the proof,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
is differentiable at
X
. Because
F\circ\tilde{\Lambda}=F\circ\Lambda
by symmetry of
F
, differentiability of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F\circ\tilde{\Lambda})\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
at
X
follows, and

\displaystyle(F\circ\tilde{\Lambda})^{\prime}(X)=(F\circ\Lambda)^{\prime}(X)=\sum_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))e_{\pi^{-1}(i)}\otimes e_{\pi^{-1}(i)}.

(32)

Since
F
is symmetric,
F\circ P_{\pi}=F
. Hence, for
x\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(x)=(F\circ P_{\pi})^{\prime}(x)=P_{\pi}^{*}F^{\prime}(P_{\pi}x)=P_{\pi}^{-1}F^{\prime}(P_{\pi}x).

Thus,
F_{i}^{\prime}(\Lambda(X))=F_{\pi^{-1}(i)}^{\prime}(P_{\pi}\Lambda(X))=F_{\pi^{-1}(i)}^{\prime}(\tilde{\Lambda}(X))
. From (
32
) it now follows that

\displaystyle(F\circ\tilde{\Lambda})^{\prime}(X)=\sum_{i}F_{\pi^{-1}(i)}^{\prime}(\tilde{\Lambda}(X))e_{\pi^{-1}(i)}\otimes e_{\pi^{-1}(i)}=\sum_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}(\tilde{\Lambda}(X))e_{i}\otimes e_{i}.

∎

See
4.20

Proof of
Proposition

4.20
.

Let
U,V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}VV^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}

and
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{X\in L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}:\ \operatorname{ran}{X}\subset\mathcal{Z}\}\subset L_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
\dim{\mathcal{Z}}<\infty
since
U
,
V
and
H
are finite-rank, and
\operatorname{ran}{g(U+tV)}\subset\mathcal{Z}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
by definition (
20
) of
g
, hence
g(U+tV)\in\mathcal{W}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
F_{f}\circ\Lambda\circ g(U+tV)={\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}\circ g(U+tV)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By
Lemma

4.13
and
Proposition

4.18
,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}
is Fréchet differentiable. By
Lemma

4.13
,
g
is Fréchet differentiable. In particular,
g
is Gateaux differentiable at
U
in the direction
V
. Hence, by the chain rule,
J_{f}
is Gateaux differentiable at
U
in the direction
V
. To compute the derivative, we recall that
(e_{j}\otimes e_{k})_{j,k}
is an ONB of
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.
The Gateaux derivative of
J_{f}
at
U
in the direction
V
is

\displaystyle J_{f}^{\prime}(U)(V)

\displaystyle=({\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}\circ g)^{\prime}(U)(V)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle{\left.\kern-1.2pt(F_{f}\circ\Lambda)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\prime}(g(U)),g^{\prime}(U)(V)\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i}f^{\prime}\left(\Lambda_{i}(g(U))\right)e_{i}\otimes e_{i},g^{\prime}(U)(V)\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k}\Big\langle\sum_{i}f^{\prime}\left(\Lambda_{i}(g(U))\right)e_{i}\otimes e_{i},e_{j}\otimes e_{k}\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}\langle g^{\prime}(U)(V),e_{j}\otimes e_{k}\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f^{\prime}\left(\Lambda_{i}(g(U))\right)\langle g^{\prime}(U)(V),e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}.

The second equation follows by the chain rule. The third equation follows from the expression for the derivative in
Proposition

4.18
applied with
X\leftarrow g(U)
and the expression for
F_{f}^{\prime}
in
Lemma

4.13
. The fourth and fifth equations use the property that
(e_{j}\otimes e_{k})_{j,k}
is an ONB of
L_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.
Using the formula for
g^{\prime}(U)(V)
from
Lemma

4.13
,

\displaystyle J_{f}^{\prime}(U)(V)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f^{\prime}\left(\Lambda_{i}(g(U))\right)\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2},e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f^{\prime}\left(\Lambda_{i}(g(U))\right)\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UV^{*}+VU^{*})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},e_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i}f^{\prime}\left(\Lambda_{i}(g(U))\right)\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},VU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle.

∎

See
4.21

Proof of
Lemma

4.21
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{F}
and let
(U_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
such that
\lVert U_{n}\rVert\rightarrow\infty
. Then, by
Lemma

A.2
,
\lVert U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\rVert=\lVert U_{n}\rVert^{2}\rightarrow\infty
. Since
f(x)\rightarrow\infty
for
\lVert x\rVert\rightarrow\infty
and
f
is bounded from below, it is enough to show that there is an eigenvalue
\alpha_{n}
of
g(U_{n})
with
\lvert\alpha_{n}\rvert\rightarrow\infty
. Since
g(U_{n})
is self-adjoint and compact by definition (
20
),
\lVert g(U_{n})\rVert=\max_{i}\lvert\Lambda_{i}(g(U_{n}))\rvert
by
Lemma

A.3
, and we must therefore show that
\lVert g(U_{n})\rVert\rightarrow\infty
. For this, it is enough to find a bounded sequence
h_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
, such that
\lVert g(U_{n})h_{n}\rVert\rightarrow\infty
.

For any
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we have by the triangle inequality, (
20
) and
Proposition

3.7

\displaystyle\lVert g(U)h\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h\rVert-\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h\rVert-1.

(33)

Let us write
m\coloneqq\dim{\mathcal{V}}
. For each
n
, let us diagonalise
U_{n}U_{n}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{n,j}\psi_{n,j}\otimes\psi_{n,j}
, where
(\psi_{n,j})_{j=1}^{m}
forms an ONB of
\mathcal{V}
and
\beta_{n,j}\geq 0
. Define
j_{n}\coloneqq\argmax_{j\leq m}\beta_{n,j}
, so that
\beta_{n,j_{n}}
is the largest eigenvalue of
U_{n}U_{n}^{*}
. As
U_{n}U_{n}^{*}
is self-adjoint,
\beta_{n,j_{n}}=\lVert U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\rVert
by
Lemma

A.3
, showing
\beta_{n,j_{n}}\rightarrow\infty
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
. By density of
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}_{\textup{pos}}}
, for each
j\leq m
we can choose
k_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
which satisfies
\lVert\psi_{1,j}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}k_{j}\rVert\leq\varepsilon
.
Let us decompose
\psi_{n,j_{n}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\psi_{1,j}\rangle\psi_{1,j}
and define
h_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\psi_{1,j}\rangle k_{j}
. Note that
\lVert h_{n}\rVert\leq C
with
C\coloneqq m\max_{j}\lVert k_{j}\rVert
by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. By further application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\lVert\psi_{n,j_{n}}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\psi_{1,j}\rangle^{2}\lVert\psi_{1,j}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}k_{j}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i\not=j}\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\psi_{1,i}\rangle\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\psi_{1,j}\rangle\langle\psi_{1,i}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}k_{i},\psi_{1,j}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}k_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq m\varepsilon^{2}+2m(m-1)\varepsilon^{2}=m(2m-1)\varepsilon^{2}.

It follows that for
\varepsilon
small enough, there exists
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\lVert\psi_{n,j_{n}}\rVert^{2}+\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rVert^{2}-\lVert\psi_{n,j_{n}}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rVert^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+0-m(2m-1)\varepsilon^{2})>c.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, the bound
\lVert h_{n}\rVert\leq C
, the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}
is self-adjoint, the given diagonalisation of
U_{n}U_{n}^{*}
and the previous lower bound,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rVert

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1}{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n},h_{n}\rangle}=C^{-1}\langle U_{n}U_{n}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=C^{-1}\sum_{j}\beta_{n,j}\lvert\langle\psi_{n,j},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rangle\rvert^{2}\geq C^{-1}\beta_{n,j_{n}}\lvert\langle\psi_{n,j_{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h_{n}\rangle\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c^{2}C^{-1}\beta_{n,j_{n}}\rightarrow\infty.

Combining this with (
33
), we thus find a bounded sequence
(h_{n})_{n}
with
\lVert g(U_{n})h_{n}\rVert\rightarrow\infty
.
Finally, by
Proposition

4.20
,
J_{f}
is differentiable. The conclusion follows because a coercive, differentiable function on a finite-dimensional space
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
has a global minimum, that is attained only among its stationary points.
∎

See
4.23

Proof of
Theorem

4.23
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{F}
. By
Proposition

4.20
,
J_{f}
is Gateaux differentiable.
It follows from
[
4
, Theorem 12.4.5 (i)]
that local minimisers of
J_{f}
are stationary points, i.e. have Gateaux derivative equal to 0.
The idea of the proof is to find among all stationary points of
J_{f}
the stationary points that minimise
J_{f}
, and use the coercivity of
J_{f}
over finite dimensional subspaces of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
to conclude that these stationary points are global minimisers. We then relate these minimisers to the solutions of
Problems

4.5
and
4.4
.

Step 1
: characterisation of the stationary points of
J_{f}
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
. Let
(\gamma_{i})_{i}
and
(e_{i})_{i}
be as in
Lemma

4.12
, so that
e_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}
and
g(U)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
.
By
Proposition

4.20
, the Gateaux derivative
J_{f}^{\prime}(g(U))\in L_{2}^{*}\simeq L_{2}
at
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
is given by

\displaystyle J_{f}^{\prime}(U)(V)=2\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},VU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle,\quad V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H}).

Thus,
U
is a stationary point of
J_{f}
if and only if for all
V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},VU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle=0.

Since
f\in\mathscr{F}
, it follows from
Lemma

4.1
(i)
that
f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})=0
if and only if
\gamma_{i}=0
.
For an arbitrary fixed
j
, if
\gamma_{j}\not=0
, this implies that
U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}=0
for a stationary point
U
, as otherwise there exists
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
such that
\langle\varphi,U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\not=0
and the choice
V=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}e_{j}\otimes\varphi
furnishes a contradiction with
U
being stationary. Indeed, in this case,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}e_{j}\otimes\varphi)U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}e_{j}\rangle\langle U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(\gamma_{j})\langle\varphi,U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}\rangle\not=0.

Hence, if
U
is a stationary point, then
\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}=0
for all
i
. Conversely, if
\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}=0
for all
i
, then for each
i
it holds that
U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}=0
or
\gamma_{i}=0
, showing
f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},VU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle=0
for all
i
and all
V
. Hence
U
is a stationary point of
J_{f}
if and only if
\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}=0
for all
i
.

Precomposing
g(U)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
with
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
and using (
20
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UU^{*}-H)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\right)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes(\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i})\right)U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2},

where the second equation follows since
(u\otimes v)AA^{*}w=u\langle AA^{*}w,v\rangle=u\langle A^{*}w,A^{*}v\rangle=(u\otimes(A^{*}v))A^{*}w
for suitable
u
,
v
,
w
, and
A
.
Thus, if
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
is a stationary point of
J_{f}
, then

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})=(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2})^{2}.

(34)

Conversely, if (
34
) holds, then
\sum_{i}\langle\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h\rangle e_{i}=0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
because (
34
) is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UU^{*}-H)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=0
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}(UU^{*}-H)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes(\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i})\right)U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}h\rangle e_{i}.

Since
(e_{i})_{i}
is an ONB, this implies
\gamma_{i}\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},h\rangle=0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
and for all
i
.
If
\gamma_{i}\not=0
for some
i
, then taking
h=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}
we get
\lVert\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rVert=0
. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\in\ker{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}}=\ker{UU^{*}}=\ker{U^{*}}
by injectivity of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
and
Lemma

A.7
, showing
\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}=0
for all
i
. Thus,
U
satisfies (
34
) if and only if
U
is a stationary point.

By injectivity of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
and
Lemma

A.8
, we have

\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}U(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}U)^{*}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left({U}\right).

Hence
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
is a non-negative and self-adjoint operator of rank at most
r
.
In particular, it has
k
many nonzero eigenvalues for some
k\leq r
.
Suppose
U
satisfies (
34
), so that the corresponding
k
eigenpairs are also eigenpairs of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}
.
By
Proposition

3.7
, there exists a nondecreasing sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}((-1,0])
with exactly
\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)
nonzero entries and ONBs
(w_{i})_{i}
and
(v_{i})_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}
with
w_{i},v_{i}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}}
and
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}
for all
i
, such that

\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=\sum_{i}(-\lambda_{i})v_{i}\otimes v_{i}
.
It follows that there exists a set of
k
distinct indices
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-\lambda_{i_{j}})v_{i_{j}}\otimes v_{i_{j}}.

(35)

Conversely, if
U
satisfies (
35
) for some distinct indices
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)\}
, then by using (
35
) and
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=\sum_{i}(-\lambda_{i})v_{i}\otimes v_{i}
and direct computation,
U
also satisfies (
34
). Thus,
U
is a stationary point if and only if there exists an index set
\mathcal{I}\subset\{1,\ldots,\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)\}
of cardinality at most
r
and containing distinct indices such that

\displaystyle UU^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}(-\lambda_{j})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}v_{j}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}v_{j}.

In particular, by
Lemma

A.8
, the stationary points
U
of
J_{f}
satisfy
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
,
where,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\operatorname{span}{\left(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}v_{i},i=1,\ldots,\operatorname{rank}\left({H}\right)\right)}\subset\mathcal{H}.

Step 2
: computation of the stationary points of
J_{f}
with minimal value of
J_{f}
.

Since
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}
for all
i
, it holds by (
11c
) that
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}
for all
i
.
Thus, if
U
is a stationary point such that the above expression of
UU^{*}
holds for the index set
\mathcal{I}
, then we have by (
20
) and (
11b
)

\displaystyle g(U)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}(-\lambda_{j})\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{j}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{j}}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{j}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{j}}}+\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}},

and hence the eigenvalues of
g(U)
form the set
\{0\}\cup\{\lambda_{i}:\ i\not\in\mathcal{I}\}\subset(-1,0]
. Since
f\in\mathscr{F}
, it holds that
f(0)=0
, and it then follows from (
21
) that
J_{f}(U)=\sum_{i\not\in\mathcal{I}}f(\lambda_{i})
.
Furthermore,
f
is decreasing on
(-1,0]
. Let
\hat{U}
be a stationary point corresponding to the index set
\mathcal{I}
. Then
\hat{U}
minimises
J_{f}
among its stationary points if and only if the sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
contains the
r
most negative elements of
(\lambda_{i})_{i}
. In turn, this is the case if and only if
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{\textup{opt}}
where
\mathcal{I}^{\textup{opt}}
is any index set for which
\{\lambda_{i}:\ i\in\mathcal{I}^{\textup{opt}}\}=\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}\}
. The set
\mathcal{I}^{\textup{opt}}
is uniquely defined if and only if
\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r+1}
, in which case
\mathcal{I}^{\textup{opt}}=\{1,\ldots,r\}
.
Thus, using once more
v_{i}=\sqrt{1+\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}
,
\hat{U}
satisfies

\displaystyle\hat{U}\hat{U}^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i},

(36)

and
J_{f}(\hat{U})=\sum_{i>r}f(\lambda_{i})
.
Now,
\hat{U}\hat{U}^{*}
is uniquely defined if and only if either
\lambda_{r+1}=0
or
\mathcal{I}^{\textup{opt}}
is uniquely defined.
Hence
\hat{U}\hat{U}^{*}
is unique if and only if either
\lambda_{r+1}=0
or
\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r+1}
.

Step 3
: identification of the stationary points with minimal value of
J_{f}
as the minimisers of
J_{f}
.

To prove that the minima of
J_{f}
are precisely the stationary points
\hat{U}
defined in
Step 2
, we first show for fixed
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
that
{\left.\kern-1.2ptJ_{f}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{U})}}^{\prime}(U)=0
implies
J_{f}^{\prime}(U)=0
. Using
Lemma

4.12
, we can diagonalise
g(U)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
with
(e_{i})_{i}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}
. Using the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}=\sum_{i}(-\lambda_{i})v_{i}\otimes v_{i}
, and using the definition of
\mathcal{V}
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{V}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}
, and by
Lemma

A.8
,
\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}=\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U}
. Thus, for each
j
for which
\gamma_{j}\not=0
, the identity
\gamma_{j}e_{j}=g(U)e_{j}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}-\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}
implies that
e_{j}\in\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}UU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}+\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}H\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}}\subset\operatorname{ran}{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}U}+\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}\mathcal{V}
. Hence
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}e_{j}\in\operatorname{ran}{U}+\mathcal{V}
. Now, by the expression of the derivative of
J_{f}
in
Proposition

4.20
,
{\left.\kern-1.2ptJ_{f}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{U})}}^{\prime}(U)=0
implies
2\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},VU^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle=0
for all
V\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{U})
. For any
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
V\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}e_{j}\otimes\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{U})
and hence
0=2\sum_{i}f^{\prime}(\gamma_{i})\langle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i},\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{-1/2}e_{j}\rangle\langle\varphi,U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}\rangle=2f^{\prime}(\gamma_{j})\langle\varphi,U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}\rangle
. Since
\gamma_{j}\not=0
,
f^{\prime}(\gamma_{j})\not=0
by
Lemma

4.1
(i)
. Thus,
U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{j}=0
. We conclude that
\gamma_{i}U^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}^{1/2}e_{i}=0
for all
i
. As was shown in
Step 1
of the proof, it then holds that
J_{f}^{\prime}(U)=0
.

By
Lemma

4.21
,
\hat{U}
minimises
J_{f}
over
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})
for the space
\mathcal{V}
defined above.
Furthermore, if
\tilde{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{H})
with
\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{U}}\not\subset\mathcal{V}
, then
\tilde{U}
is not a stationary point of
J_{f}
, because a necessary condition for
\tilde{U}
to be a stationary point of
J_{f}
is that
\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{U}}\subset\mathcal{V}
, by
Step 1
. By the previous paragraph,
\tilde{U}
is not a stationary point of
J_{f}
restricted to
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{U}})
. Since
\hat{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V})\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{U}})
, since
\tilde{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{U}})
and since
J_{f}
is coercive over
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}+\operatorname{ran}{\tilde{U}})
by
Lemma

4.21
, it follows that
J_{f}(\tilde{U})>J_{f}(\hat{U})
. Thus,
\hat{U}
is a global minimiser of
J_{f}
.

Step 4
: identification of the solutions of
Problems

4.5
and
4.4
.

Since
J_{f}(\hat{U})=\mathcal{L}_{f}(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pos}}\|(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+\hat{U}\hat{U}^{*})^{-1})
by (
18
), it follows that the operator
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
in (
22
) solves
Problem

4.5
.
By
Corollary

4.10
(ii)
,
(\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})^{-1}
solves
Problem

4.4
. It remains to show that
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
defined in (
23
) satisfies
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}=(\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})^{-1}
. Since taking the inverse is a bijective operation, uniqueness of
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
is then implied by uniqueness of
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}
. Now, by (
22
),
Lemma

A.9
with
\delta_{i}\leftarrow\lambda_{i}
, and (
23
),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{\textup{opt}}_{r})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}w_{i}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\left(I-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{-\lambda_{i}}{1+\lambda_{i}}w_{i}\otimes w_{i}\right)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{-1/2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}\left(I+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}w_{i}\otimes w_{i}\right)\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}(-\lambda_{i})\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\textup{pr}}^{1/2}w_{i}=\mathcal{C}^{\textup{opt}}_{r}.

∎
